Key Developments

Yeltsin Wants Democratic Majority in Next Duma

LD1110182695 Moscow ITAR-TASS in English
1737 GMT 11 Oct 95

{By Konstantin Kolesov and Andrey Storkh]

(FBIS Transcribed Text] MOSCOW, Oct 11 (ITAR-
TASS) — “The President is clearly in control of the
pre-election situation and he is well informed about
campaigning in the country. He has unequivocally de-
termined that the democratic forces should have a ma-
jority in the State Duma," chairman of the Prime Min-
ister’s election alliance “Our Home is Russia" Sergey
Belyayev [position as received] said after today’s meet-
ing with President Boris Yeltsin.

Asked whether the President continued to believe that
the movement would gain between eight and twelve
percent of all votes in the elections for the State Duma,
Belyayev said that since "the President is a guarantor
of concord in society, he cannot give open preference
to one or another movement,” but he nonetheless set a
clear task to us — “to form a democratic majority.”

Belyayev said society was in a vague state containing
radicals, left-wingers and centrist and right-of-centre
democrats. Belyayev said that the democratic wing,
including supporters of the movement "Our Home is
Russia,” embraces “a broad centre” oriented towards
implementing reforms which the President began and
the Government has continued.

Asked whether the President expressed his own political
sympathies during his meeting with the leaders of
the movement “Our Home is Russia" on Wednesday,
Belyayev said “it is easier to list all those whom the
President does not accept. Undoubtedly, they include
the communist party (and we regard all attacks on the
President by the CPRF [Communist Party of the Russian
Federation] as a negative phenomenon for our society),
the Agrarian party that echoes the communists, and all
the fragments of the Communist party of the Soviet
Union and other former communist organisations which
now represent the left wing of the political spectrum.
They are not our friends, they are our most serious
adversaries in the elections. And Boris Nikolayevich has
never supported the Liberal Democratic party of Russia.
As regards the Congress of Russian communities, we
did not touch on it in the course of our meeting.”

DUMA ELECTION ISSUES

Grachev Issues Statement on Not Running for
Duma

LD1210180495 Moscow ITAR-TASS World Service
in Russian 1645 GMT 12 Oct 95

{[FBIS Translated Text] Moscow, 12 Oct (ITAR-TASS)
— Russian Defense Minister Pavel Grachev made the
following statement today:

"In connection with reports appearing in the media on
my intention to stand for election in a number of con-
stituencies, I believe it necessary to provide the fol-
lowing clarifications. I have indeed received numerous
appeals from the working and military collectives and
public organizations urging me to agree to be nomi-
nated as a Duma candidate. However, I never intended
to stand and am not planning to do so.

"I should like to express my gratitude to all those who
proposed my nomination. I view this, first of all, as an
expression of trust and respect toward the army and the
navy and the recognition of the prestige of the armed
forces, which are fulfilling the difficult task of protecting
the national interests of the state."

ITAR-TASS received the text of Pavel Grachev’s state-
ment from the public relations officer of Russian defense
minister.

Lebed Denies Split in KRO, Urges Strong Army

MMI1210085795 Moscow TRUD in Russian
11 Oct 95 pp 1, 4

{Interview with Lieutenant General Aleksandr Lebed,
one of the leaders of the "Congress of Russian Com-
munities," KRO, electoral association and former 14th
Army commander, by Sergey Paviov; place and date
not given: "Aleksandr Lebed: You Can’t Have a Strong
Russia Without a Strong Army" — first paragraph is
TRUD introduction]

(FBIS Translated Text] Aleksandr Lebed, as commander
of the 14th Army in the Dniester Region, always had the
reputation of being the general in the Russian Army who
was most accessible to the press. After leaving his post
as commander of the 14th Army and becoming one of
the leaders of the "Congress of Russian Com:nunities"
[KRO] electoral association, General Lebed continued
his active contacts with the press. However, recently
he has changed his habits, and getting an interview
with him has become a big problem. At the same time
rumors are proliferating to the effect that there are
difficulties in the relationship between Lebed and the
other KRO leader, Yuriy Skokov, and that the Kremlin
intends to launch some kind of military movement in
support of army reform, for which Lebed himself is
tipped as leader. And according to some reports this

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

2 DUMA ELECTION ISSUES

movement will not necessarily be part of the KRO.
Our correspondent managed to get an interview with
Lieutenant General Aleksandr Lebed.

[Pavlov] Aleksandr Ivanovich, what, in your view, is
the present state of the Russian Army?

[Lebed] The Soviet Army was a strong army, for all
its many shortcomings. That must be admitted. It was
an education. But from 1988 onward, the principle of
combined [military and political] command disappeared
in the Soviet Army, and later this phenomenon was
carried over smoothly into the Russian Army. The
Army was used to plug political holes and cover up
blunders, and in the process it was constantly blamed.
The withdrawal of the Army, without any kind of
plan, from the territory of Hungary, Czechoslovakia,
and Poland was a disaster. Officers were shoved into
henhouses, barns, and barracks. People lost all hope.
Units with their own history that had raised generations
of servicemen were disbanded first of all. This was no
accident, it was, if you like, a malicious system. But I
have to say you can’t have a strong Russia without a
strong Army and Navy.

[Pavlov] Do you reject the idea of democratization of
the Army?

[Lebed] I can’t imagine a democratized army. If one
person has the right to send another into battle, what
kind of democracy can you have there? Nobody wants to
die, after all. We can and must take care of servicemen’s
social protection and give them a decent life. But a
democratic army isn’t an army, it’s something else. We
need a civilized army in which servicemen’s interests
are protected. Also, under present laws you can’t draft
students, you can’t draft elite children, not elite children,
but the children of elite parents. What we have now is
not a law on universal military service, but a law on
universal military exemption. The Army has become a
“worker-peasant" army, and that has led to a decline
in its intellectual level. But a strong army has great
intellectual potential.

[Pavlov] Let’s imagine you became defense minister.
What would you do first?

[Lebed] Whatever I say, it’s going to be seen on the
basis of the principle: “Everyone pictures himself as
a strategist when he’s watching the battle from the
sidelines." If I became that strategist, 1 would do what
needs to be done for the country. There is and has been
no reform in the Army. There has been nothing but
flimflam. Four years ago it was possible to talk about
a gradual reform of the Army, but now we must talk
about building it rather than reforming it. The Army
has largely been smashed up by Grachev’s own efforts.

FBIS-SOV-95-198
13 October 1995

The job has gotten more difficult. But we still have an
army, even if it is half-ruined. It can’t be restored in a
short time, but the most important things could be done
in 1-1.5 years. You could eliminate all those mongrel
reduced-strength units with 30 officers and two men.
Let’s have 10 times fewer divisions, but combine them
to form expanded formations. They should each have
15,000 men, a modern training system, a dual or triple
arms basing system, and a well-oiled redeployment
system. And combat training!

A strong army is not one that fights, but one that by the
very fact of its existence destroys ideas of aggression.
Let’s have troops whose existence means that nobody
will want to tangle with us. And this should be under
the protection of the nuclear shield. That is the only
counterweight, given the existence of nuclear weapons
in the United States, Britain, France, and China. It
must also be taken into account that while there are
common problems, there are also the problems of each
specific garrison. It is a huge country, so an army reform
program cannot be the fruit of just one man’s work,
however smart he may be. This job must be done by
collective brain power. There are enormous numbers of
intelligent officers who were prematurely discharged.
They have plenty of ideas, they are ready to serve for
free, but they can’t put their ideas into practice. They
need a focus. That’s why I intend in the near future to
set up a public military movement, it’s realistic.

On the question of military organizational development
we should take into account both the tactical and the
Strategic level — what we can achieve over a period
of 20-30 years. Here we should proceed on the basis
of Russia’s national interests. We have a state with
“displaced borders," as we are reminded by, for instance,
Kaliningrad, the Dniester Region, Tajikistan.... And the
state must declare, through the mouths of its political
leaders, that this line is the line of state interests. But
as soon as you have interests, you also get threats to
those interests. The system of counteraction is realized
through the creation of the corresponding groupings,
airfields, etc. That is the starting point. But today
we have a country without interests, without enemies,
without friends, without traditions. And a strong army
is a repository of tradition.

[Pavlov] What threats do you see to Russia's security?

[Lebed] Russia must be defended! Let’s start with the
East. China. A territory about one-third the size of ours,
yet the population today is 1.2 billion. The prediction
for the year 2010 is 1.6 billion. One day they'll simply
no longer fit into their own territory. Shouldn't we
bear in mind this prospect and the possibility of a
potential danger to our interests arising? There is the

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-95-198
13 October 1995

Islamic world, which we have not always been on good
terms with recently. Our actions in Afghanistan and
Chechnya affected this. Incidentally, Chechnya was a
pretext for the stepping up of the third military threat
factor — NATO expansion. Russia today is incapable of
confronting not only all three threats at once, but even
any one of them individually. Our economy is in ruins,
our Army is half in ruins, and the main thing is that
people see no glimmer of light. The world balance is
important. Any system tends toward equilibrium. And
there is nothing stopping us becoming strong again.
Not through heightened aggressiveness. It’s true, ours
is a self-sufficient country, we have everything. We
don’t need to borrow from anyone. We have our own
autonomous economic system. And we have enough of
a population, enough bright people, to live by our wits.

[Pavlov] How should Moscow’s relations with the
Federation components be structured?

[Lebed] The processes that wrecked the USSR have now
been carried over to Russia’s territory. Once there were
the union republics, now they have become independent
states. And there are 23 national territorial formations
in Russia’s territory now. If they are separated off, the
result will be a blanket full of holes.

[Pavlov] What do you see as the way to solve the
Chechen problem?

{Lebed}] I have the impression that in Chechnya a
frankly criminal, mafia regime was being lovingly
nurtured, and until a certain point that regime paid
up. When it realized that it was strong enough, that
it had grown up and could tell its elder brother to get
lost, it stopped paying up. It had to be punished. But
how could you punish it? Only by using the Army, the
police isn’t going to be any use. So they sent in the
Army. Whereas they should have adopted political and
economic measures of influence, closed the airspace,
surrounded the borders, cut off the flow of oil.

[Pavlov] So you suggest imposing an economic block-
ade?

[Lebed] Why put it so crudely? Just give people the
opportunity to feel the burden of independence.
[Pavlov] Would you grant them political independence?
[Lebed] No.

[Pavlov] Why are you in the KRO specifically?

{Lebed] I can sense the growing strength of this
movement. This organization grew up on the basis
of the Russian communities of the near abroad and
was supposed to coordinate their actions ard give
them direct moral and spiritual support. Now it is a
broader association. There are democrats, socialists, and

DUMA ELECTION ISSUES 3

communists within it. Personally, I don’t care who you
are, what you consider yourself to be. If you want to
come with us, come. You might be an atheist or a
believer. Let’s get on with the job. You must recognize
the mighty force that goes by the name of "my Russian
people." Russians in Russia are 85 percent of the
population, so why car’t we live by our own ideas in our
own country and why can’t we start building our lives
according to our own plan? At the same time, naturally,
we must build civilized relations with all the peoples of
Russia. We have been living together for 1,000 years,
and we have to go on living together, and so one day,
after all, we will arrive at the concept "citizen of Russia"
["rossiyanin"]. Take the Americans. An American can
be of Chinese, Japanese, or Russian extraction, but he’s
still an American.

[Pavlov] Many people are now talking about political
differences within the KRO, in particular your differ-
ences with Skokov.

[Lebed] They’ ve been talking, and they’ll go on talking.
This is one of the attempts to bring about a split in
the movement. There are no differences of principle
between us. We have known each other a long time, for
more than four years we’ve known each other well. I
have my own personal criteria. There are three of them:
my relations with people after August 1991, the summer
of 1992, and the summer of 1995. Those were my three
moments. And, judged on these three criteria, Yuriy
Vladimirovich [Skokov] is beyond reproach. When you
are close to power, when you're strong, you always
have a lot of friends. But when things are going badly
for you, only your true friends remain. On these three
criteria Skokov suits me absolutely fine.

National Republican Party Fails To Gain Allies

LD0910154495 Moscow INTERFAX in English
1436 GMT 9 Oct 95

(FBIS Transcribed Text) MOSCOW, Oct 9 (INTER-
FAX) — The National-Republican Party of Russia,
headed by Duma deputy Nikolay Lysenko, has failed
to gain allies in the election campaign. It will partici-
pate in the elections on its own, political secretary of
the party Nikolay Pavlov told an exclusive INTERFAX
interview Monday.

In his opinion, the progress of signature collection
(100,000 signatures have been collected) inspires hopes
the Party will have no less than 5% of votes in the
elections of December 17, 1995.

Pavlov said he does not take into account the 30,000
Signatures taken away in an armed attack at the party
headquarters in St. Petersburg on September 28.

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

4 DUMA ELECTION ISSUES

Speaking of the party’s possible allies, the political
secretary said "the recent consultations revealed that the
party will not act together with the Russian all-People’s
Union of Sergey Baburin or the election bloc of Power
to the People of Nikolay Ryzhkov "because they are far
from the genuine Russian national movement.”

Regional branches of the party, which has about 12,000
members, have been formed in 36 Russian areas.
Ex-deputy of the parliament of the Russian Soviet
Federative Socialist Republic Nikolay Paviov, who
was a leader of the National Salvation Front and the
opposition till October 1993, runs on the top of the
party’s list of candidates to the Duma together with
Lysenko.

Election Guidelines

CEC Approves Additions to Edict on Electoral
Blocs

964FO0SIA Moscow ROSSIYSKAYA GAZETA
in Russian 27 Sep 95 p4

[Additions to the "Explanations to Procedures for Form-
ing Electoral Blocs, Their Registration by the Central
Electoral Commission of the Russian Federation, and
the Nomination of Candidates for Deputy by an Elec-
toral Bloc")

(FBIS Translated Text] The Central Electoral Commis-
sion [CEC] of the Russian Federation [RF] in Decree
No. 17/146-II, dated 18 September 1995, approved ad-
ditions to the "Explanations on Procedures on Forming
Electoral Blocs, Their Registration by the RF CEC, and
the Nomination of Candidates for Deputy by an Elec-
toral Bloc,” approved by RF CEC Decree No. 8/50-II
dated 28th July 1995.

An electoral bloc’s registration by the RF CEC does not
preclude the possibility of it subsequently changing its
composition in the future, by reason of more associa-
tions joining the electoral bloc or by reason of one or
several other associations dropping out of it.

The RF CEC will revoke the decision on an electoral
bloc’s registration if, after the electoral bloc’s registra-
tion is carried out but before certified copies of can-
didate lists are issued to the electoral bloc’s autho-
rized representative, the following are submitted to the
commission: when joining a bloc—relevant decisions
of congresses (conferences) of public associations and
a joint decision of all public associations to create an
electoral bloc in a new composition, and when leaving

FBIS-SOV-95-198
13 October 1995

a bloc—televant decisions of congresses (conferences)
or their authorized leadership bodies.

Public associations, both those that set up a bloc in the
new composition and those that decide to remain in the
bloc after one or several public associations leave it,
have the right to submit to the RF CEC in keeping with
established procedures the documents necessary for the
electoral bloc’s registration, enumerated in part four of
Article 33 of the federal law “On elections of deputies to
the State Duma of the Federal Assembly of the Russian
Federation.”

At the same time, with respect to public associations that
were previously part of an electoral bloc and confirm the
decision to remain in the bloc in its new composition,
they may submit protocols of congresses (conferences)
with the decision to join the electoral bloc that was
submitted to the RF CEC in order to register the bloc
that was subsequently revoked.

A joint decision of public associations on creating an
electoral bloc in a new composition (retaining the bloc
after one or more public associations drop out) must
be signed by authorized representatives of only those
public associations that remain in it.

RF CEC decisions on revoking an electoral bloc’s
registration and on registering an electoral bloc in a new
composition may be made at the same meeting of the
commission.

An electoral bloc’s composition may be changed even
after its authorized representative receives certified
copies of lists of candidates nominated by the electoral
bloc. In this case, the electoral bloc’s registration is
done in keeping with the requirements and procedures
envisaged for an electoral bloc’s registration during
the period before its authorized representative is issued
certified copies of lists of candidates. f

Changing an electoral bloc’s composition by reason of
additional public associations joining it voids the legal
validity of voters’ signatures in support of the federal list
of candidates and candidates nominated by this electoral
bloc in single-seat districts collected before the RF CEC
issues a decision on registering the electoral bloc in a
new composition.

However, voters’ signatures collected by an electoral
bloc whose change in composition and registration in
a new composition were caused by public associations
dropping out of it do not lose their legal validity.

Over the period after the registration by the RF CEC of
the list of candidates nominated by the electoral bloc,
its registration in a new composition is only possible
by reason of public associations dropping out of the

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-95-198
13 October 1995

electoral bloc, but no later than five days before the
elections.

Forecasts & Commentary

Shumeyko Interviewed on Elections, Local Politics,
Budget

MMI1210085595 Moscow ROSSIYSKAYA GAZETA

in Russian 10 Oct 95 pp 1, 3

{Interview with Russian Federation Council Chairman
Vladimir Shumeyko “prepared by" Mikhail Gurevich
and conducted at ROSSIYSKAYA GAZETA editorial
office on 2 October: “Three Pillars of Strong Gov-
ernment: Center, Regions, Party" — first paragraph is
ROSSIYSKAYA GAZETA introduction]

(FBIS Translated Text] Sessions of the Russian Feder-
ation Federation Council resumed 3 October after the
summer recess. But the vacation ended earlier than that
for Federation Council Chairman Vladimir Shumeyko.
The previous day he came to the ROSSIYSKAYA
GAZETA editorial office. The top senator’s conversa-
tion with journalists was very meaty. Judge for your-
selves.

[ROSSIYSKAYA GAZETA] What is your view of the
current “upsurge” in press interest in you?

[Shumeyko] It is an entirely justified “upsurge.” After
all, the election campaign is under way. Unfortunately,
in our country it is proceeding according to the princi-
ple: “The further into the forest you go, the muddier it
gets." Our unforgettable Ruslan Imranovich [Khasbula-
tov] put it like this: “Politics is a dirty business." Admit-
tedly, he himself made considerable efforts to vindicate
this thesis.

Although I am not taking a direct part in the election
campaign and am a member of no bloc, my role as
chairman of the Federation Council, with a strong
influence on what is happening with the Duma elections,
is very clear. When all these polls assessing influence
are published, I always ask just one question: “What I
would like to know is: Where, when, on whom, and on
what decisions does this politician exert influence?” I am
perfectly aware of where and on whom I exert influence.
And the publishers of the polls also know this. So it is
all quite natural at this stage of the elections.

Without Middlemen

{[ROSSIYSKAYA GAZETA] It is well known that
you do nothing simply for its own sake. You recently
went to Yekaterinburg for the founding congress of the
“Transformation of the “atherland” bloc. And the press
again began to speculate: The Kremlin is trying to turn

DUMA ELECTION ISSUES 15

the party of regional leaders into a counterweight to the
“Russia Is Our Home" movement.

([Shumeyko] I would answer this question in broader
terms. Let us analyze what is happening today with
the Duma elections from the viewpoint of ordinary
statistics and formal logic. Sixty electoral associations
have already been registered. Experts from the Central
Electoral Commission say that there will be around
70 or even more. When it comes to the federal lists,
there are now 40 contenders per seat, and there are 20
contenders per seat in the single-seat districts. This is
quite unprecedented!

The deadline for collecting signatures is 22 October, and
I hope that fewer contenders will be left and that not
everyone will surmount the first barrier — collecting
5,000 signatures for themselves personally and 200,000
per association. But a simple calculation already shows
that the single-seat districts — these are the only ones
that interest me at present — will have between seven
and 12 people per seat. That is also a great many.

Let us divide all the contenders into three groups. The
first group is those who are connected with major blocs
and associations — "Russia Is Our Home," "Democratic
Choice," the CPRF [Communist Party of the Russian
Federation], the Agrarians — and with those who are
currently in the Duma. The second is those who are
supported by the local authorities. And the third is those
who are supported by commercial organizations, and
experts testify that up to 10 percent of them will be
directly connected with criminal formations.

When we discussed the law on elections to the State
Duma in the Federation Council we bluntly said: It
would be best for all the deputies to be elected according
to the first-past-the-post system. We don’t want party
lists, we want people elected directly in the district
because of their personal qualities.

I read a comment in one newspaper: "All the deputies
from the regions will come along and defend only
regional interests."

But what interests does Russia have besides regional
interests?

There are no purely party interests — these are in fact
group interests. A specific group rather than the whole of
society is behind even the biggest association — “Russia
Is Our Home." But it should be everybody. Because
the most important thing for the state today is for it
to develop the way it is developing — federally. So
far as I am concerned democracy is inseparable from
federalism.

is report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

16 DUMA ELECTION ISSUES

I am most interested in the second group in my
classification. So far as I am concerned this is one of
the main criteria — whether a deputy is supported by
the local authorities. This usually coincides with the
opinion of people themselves. I agree that in Russia
it is bad to belong to the authorities at all. But if you
compare the local authorities and the central authorities
— for people who live in the regions — statistics show
that the local authorities are trusted 27 percent more.
And people know that these are their own authorities,
they defend them. Whereas the central authorities, by
contrast, are "alien" and are responsible for everything
bad. Such is the mentality.

[Sverdlovsk Governor] Rossel’s “Transformation of
the Fatherland" movement is not the most powerful
movement, and I said as much at their congress. But
its value is that it was not formed to suit a central
leader. Suppose Shumeyko decided to create his own
movement — there is no question, it would spring
up overnight. And it would be of no value. But this
movement emerged from below — it developed from
"Transformation of the Urals" into “Transformation of
the Fatherland." They have different tasks and different
aims. And the Federation Council must support regional
deputies without middlemen in the shape of a Moscow
party-political coterie. Any middlemen only worsen the
process and, as is well known, make it more expensive.

But participation in congresses and visits are just the
first stage. There will also be a second stage — when
all the signatures have been collected and it is clear who
is left. I have quite a lot of ideas about how to give them
real, all-around support. The more regional deputies
there are in the Duma, the better. But there are no
particularly cheerful prospects, the Duma will probably
be a patchwork and nobody will have a majority. Hence
a different issue arises. The Federation Council must
be formed from heads of administrations and heads of
legislative assemblies from the regions and nobody else.
I am sticking to my view. Otherwise in addition to the
unpredictable Duma that may result we may have a
similar Federation Council — and then, as the joke says,
we can melt Hershey bars [allusion unknown]. I don’t
want to do that.

[ROSSIYSKAYA GAZETA] Have you had offers to
join a bloc? Or to create your own?

[Shumeyko] There have actually been misunderstand-
ings: We are all members of something, but you are not
a member of anything? But now everybody has calmed
down, everybody realizes that the chairman of the Fed-
eration Council cannot espouse any other position. They
say to me: “You are cutting off the branch you are sitting
on. The elections would be more to your advantage." All

FBIS-SOV-95-198
13 October 1995

right, I am sawing away. But that is totally beside the
point. The point is the development of the state as such.
I have spent five years in the very thick of this. I know
everybody. I know who has done what, how, and for
what purpose. And this gives me the right to behave in
the way I am behaving. Freely and independently. You
asked about the reason for these "surges" in the press.
They really hate it when someone has their own opin-
ion. You simply have to be a member of some bloc. In
all these five years I have not been a member of any
bloc and I have not joined any coterie. And in general
I have never listened to anyone. It is easier to bury me
than change me.

[ROSSIYSKAYA GAZETA] What do you mean, you
have never listened to anyone? What about the presi-
dent?

[Shumeyko] The president is a different matter. The
president is the head of state. Whether it’s Yeltsin,
Ivanov, or Petrov, Shumeyko will always work for the
head of state. To build democracy today is to strengthen
power. Anarchy cannot be permitted.

Just look at events: As soon as the situation changes,
half the people who shouted that they were in favor of
the president are no longer in favor of the president. As
for Shumeyko, he has never shouted anywhere, from
any rooftop, that he is in favor of Yeltsin, has never
taken him anywhere or done anything for him. He has
worked for the authorities, is working for them, and will
continue to work for them.

My whole life — from lathe operator to director — took
place in a large enterprise, and I always knew: “If there
is no authority, nothing will work out." But things were
simpler at the plant, of course. If anyone had tried not to
fulfill my instructions, he would simply have been gone
the next day. But so far as the president is concerned,
just look what is happening. Loads of edicts are not
being fulfilled at all, and there are officials who say:
"So what if the president has signed an edict. I know
better." It is not a lack of respect, it is just that the
very authority is lacking. But democracy is a form of
authority. And authority must be strong no matter what
it is called: monarchy or democracy.

At first people in our country took for democracy ab-
solutely anything you like except democracy. Freedom
of speech, action, and assembly are all indicators of
a developed society, but not of authority. Authority is
something else.

Beloved Country

[ROSSIYSKAYA GAZETA] I would like to ask you
as an advocate of strong authority a regional question.

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-95-198
13 October 1995

Three equal Federation components have emerged on
the territory of Tyumen Oblast — the oblast itself, and
the Khanty-Mansiysk and Yamalo-Nenets Autonomous
Okrugs. Three vertical power structures.

[Shumeyko] When the constitution was being writ-
ten (and I attended the Constitutional Conference and
headed the group of industrialists and entrepreneurs) we
discussed this question many times. From both the eco-
nomic viewpoint and the state management viewpoint
our Federation is not the best. It is called a symmetri-
cal federation, where all the components are absolutely
equal in status vis-a-vis the center. This is enshrined in
the constitution. This is one of the worst options. It was
necessary in order to preserve Russia’s unity and pre-
vent separatism.

Three components on a single territory is what is
called a “nested component.” There is one lot of land
and mineral resources but four managers: the federal
authorities and three equal components. It is a complex
question. Yes, measures must be taken. We must move
in the direction of amalgamation.

Not all Federation components — I am aware what I
am saying — are today actually Federation components.
Some arose artificially — precisely in order to ensure
symmetry, equal rights, and the country’s unity. Now
there must certainly be a process of transformation.

The constitution contains an excellent mechanism: Any
Federation component can delegate powers to the cen-
ter, the center can delegate powers to a component, and
the same thing can also take place between components.
By gradually delegating powers on the basis of a treaty
process Tyumen Oblast can be transformed — not to-
day, nor maybe even tomorrow — into a single compo-
nent. The most important point is that an economic in-
frastructure is required for this. It is impossible to deal
with politics in isolation from economics, Lenin was
absolutely right about that: Politics is the concentrated
expression of economics.

I am a candidate of technical sciences, but I took up my
pen, and my book "Russian Reforms and Federalism.
Socioeconomic Essays” will come out shortly. I spent
two years putting it together. In it I attempt to examine
problems of federalism not from the ethnic or state
viewpoint, but solely from the economic viewpoint. To
examine how Moscow exerts influence here, and what
are the bad results and the good results of the reforms
devised in the center and carried out at local level.
Sometimes totally without consideration of the regional
element. The 89 regions are totally different in terms
of all conditions and parameters. In my view, there is a
lack of theory. There is simply none. Whereas there was
theory when we were building communism before, there

DUMA ELECTION ISSUES 17

were hundreds of thousands of lecturers on Marxism-
Leninism alone in the country.

([ROSSIYSKAYA GAZETA] In any state power begins
in villages, settlements, and districts — both power and
the whole of life. In your view, do the new authorities
now exist at this level? Do the people who are in daily
contact with them accept them?

{[Shumeyko] In general terms what are the authorities?
They can give something or take something. When the
authorities give something or allocate it, they are viewed
as the authorities. If we compare things with the past
— where did the people used to live? We all used to
live in sectors of industry. What ministry had what —
that is what determined our lives. And the system of
soviets permeated the entire country. It distributed what
was given.

Today we have local self-government. According to
the constitution, it is separated from the state. Does it
constitute the authorities today? Not at present. They
cannot be the authorities since they have nothing. There
is no power without money.

Why, when examining the draft budget for 1996, do
the Duma and our committees today intend to reject
it? Because it does not enshrine the law on local self-
government. It is paradoxical: The law on local self-
government has been adopted, it provides a great deal
of scope, but unless the budget immediately defines
guidelines for people at local level, all this power will
be left hanging in the air, it will not be power.

Why are the local authorities more trusted? Because,
since they have the local budget, they do more to solve
people’s problems. Masses of material laws are adopted
at federal level which say: "Via local resources.” Teach-
ers, doctors, pensioners, and veterans are perfectly well
aware: The people at the top will not hand out money.
But the local authorities look for it — by increasing
taxes and by other means — and hand it out. That is
why they are the authorities today and they are trusted.

[ROSSIYSKAYA GAZETA] What view do the local
authorities take of the changes occurring in the state?

{Shumeyko] Would they choose the present political
and economic system? They would. I have not seen
anybody today who wants to return to a different form
of government. But we must clearly make up our minds
here.

We used to have centralized power and its mode of
existence — the planned command economy. We chose
the power called democratic, which has only one mode
of existence — the market, open economy. If you want

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

18 DUMA ELECTION ISSUES

to build democracy, you have to want to build the
market. Love me, love my child.

But how is the market to be built? Here too we “run up
against" the local authorities. They are more pragmatic
people.

They too want to build democracy, but national interests
cannot be forgotten. The aggregate of all the regions’
interests constitutes national interests.

From this viewpoint, excuse me, but I could not give
a damn whether our market is theoretically pure or not
and what kind of stabilization there is going to be there
— mild or firm. The most important thing is for this to
correspond to the development that is under way in the
State.

Deputy From the Baltic

Why do many people fail to understand Moscow’s
ideas: This is the only thing for us to do, not anything
else? Anatoliy Borisovich Chubays — I like and respect
him, he is one of the few intelligent people there are,
so to speak — declares from the platform that packages
of shares in 29 enterprises are going on sale. These
enterprises are in 13 regions. There has been preliminary
discussion with just one head of administration. The rest
learn of this from the newspapers and object. Do you
want this kind of politics to go ahead? And everybody
simply to salute?

[ROSSIYSKAYA GAZETA] You are the Federation
Council deputy from Kaliningrad Oblast. What are
things there like at the moment? The oblast has a unique,
very advantageous position, but, since the law "On the
Kaliningrad Oblast Special Economic Zone" has passed
only its first reading, inefficient use is being made of
it as yet and a lot of money that could be Russia’s is
going to Lithuania, for instance.

[Shumeyko] The Duma is still holding hearings about
this matter. Two stumbling blocks remain: flank arma-
ments and what our Foreign Ministry has in treaties
with Germany. Duma deputies are very seriously inter-
ested in these two questions. A myth has emerged: The
oblast’s economy cannot be developed so long as there
are armed forces there. It is impossible, it is argued, to
develop a free zone in a militarized zone. This myth
must be debunked. At these hearings it should calmly
be proved, with pencil and paper, that the presence of
such a small number of troops on the oblast’s territory
is no hindrance to having a free zone. This law in no
way specifies what kind of free zone is wanted: a free
customs and banking zone or a free production zone? It
ought to be comprehensive, because Kaliningrad Oblast
is located separately from Russia.

FBIS-SOV-95-198
13 October 1995

They say: What is the point in making a law for
Kaliningrad Oblast? Then the others will want one too.
But the others will not want one, because of where the
oblast is — you have to go 300 km across Lithuania
to get there. Nobody else is in this position. I think the
law will eventually emerge. And until it does, you are
absolutely right: We are losing out on a colossal scale.
We could have organized a very great many normal
links via this gateway right in the center of Europe.
Russia’s only remaining ice-frec port is on the Baltic —
we must make intensive use of it. We will find ourselves
in tough competition with Klajpeda and other ports in
all the Baltic countries. There is a need for the law.

{[ROSSIYSKAYA GAZETA] You are chairman of the
Federation Council and chairman of the CIS Interpar-
liamentary Assembly. What is your biggest headache at
present in each capacity?

[Shumeyko] In the Federation Council, the biggest
headache is the 1996 budget. It encapsulates all the
problems, all the contradictions.

In the Interparliamentary Assembly the headaches are
not so bad, but they do exist. I would like all the CIS
States to be represented in it. Thank God, there has
been a shift in Ukraine. Moroz is an ardent advocate of
Ukraine joining the Interparliamentary Assembly. So far
Uzbekistan has not joined. If everyone was represented
in the Interparliamentary Assembly, things would be
rather simpler, of course.

We are now trying to adopt model laws. While Russia
has adopted only the first part of the Civil Code, the
Interparliamentary Assembly has adopted both the first
and second parts. Ratification of the second part is
already under way in many countries. It appears that we
are drawing closer together not at the political level, but
at the economic and legislative levels. We are drawing
closer quietly, without noise and dust, so to speak. But
everyone realizes that the closer economic integration
is, the sooner political integration will begin.

Eminences Arise in a Gray Economy

[ROSSIYSKAYA GAZETA] The other branches of
power have now been joined by a "fifth" branch: the
criminal one.

[Shumeyko] I have already mentioned the 10 percent
of candidates from criminal structures. But I would be
very careful about the word “crime.” What does it mean?
There is utterly black crime that can only be combated:
weapons, drugs, murder. This has no right to exist in any
society. There is also another type — crime in relation to
the state’s legislative infrastructure. For instance, there
is no border between Russia and Azerbaijan. There is

DUMA ELECTION ISSUES 19

the Republic of Dagestan between us. It is a Russian
but next door, across a small river, is not

{[ROSSIYSKAYA GAZETA] There is also the follow-
ing point. Previously, it was said, if you worked for
three years as a [plant) director they would be able to
throw you in jail: You would be bound to have com-
mitted some kind of offense.

(Shumeyko] Yes, because everything was prohibited
and it was altogether impossible to work if you complied
with everything. So we have to investigate what crime
means. If, heaven forbid, people connected with "black"
crime become deputies, they must immediately be swept
aside. You cannot devise any solution except destruction
here. But “economic” crime is known as the "gray
economy” in the West. And Western theoreticians tell
us: "What do you think you are up to? This is bound
to happen during the transition to the market, you have
to turn a blind eye to the ‘gray’ economy, it creates
the economic infrastructure of the state." You explain
to them that our morality and our entire history make
it impossible to turn a blind eye. They cannot believe
it. But this negative attitude is actually genetically built
into us.

I also “come under fire" for this — the fact that I have
never taken a kopek — in all the newspapers. The
fact that I have not taken anything really bugs some
people.... So they have to write that I have been on the
take. They wrote that in Kaliningrad I have a dacha in
Kurshskaya Kosa. You would have to be a real idiot to
build a dacha in a prohibited zone.

A representative of crime — absolutely any sort —
becoming a legislator is crazy. You cannot divide crime
into black, gray, and white here. Both society and you,
the press, must keep an eye on who is seeking to become
a deputy.

Lofty Indeed

{[ROSSIYSKAYA GAZETA] Does the counterweight
in the form of strong regional power strengthen central
power?

{[Shumeyko] Of course it does. Power is a system. The
system must be totally balanced. It is not for nothing

that the three branches of power (admittedly they do
not now carry equal weight) are separated according to
the law. What is regional power in today’s democratic
and federal Russia? If the Federation Counci. consists
of the heads of executive and legislative power from
the regions it is a proper counterweight to the president
and the government. Not counteraction, but a balance of
power. The system of power becomes more effective.
You can go to the Federation Council and immediately
learn the reaction of the whole of Russia, as represented
by persons in authority.

I have already mentioned my book. It is a critical book.
I do not name any names, but I name phenomena: what
we wanted and what we have got. But I am entitled to
do this, for five years I myself have been involved in
this together with everyone. I am not interested in the
reaction to the criticism — it will be very harsh. But if
you are to remain a honest man, you have to act this
way. I cannot do otherwise.

([ROSSIYSKAYA GAZETA] Another book came out
recently — by your colleague Oleg Poptsov, called
"Chronicles of the Time of ‘Czar Boris.’" It says the
following about you: “He took too literally Yeltsin's
remark about his presumed successor: “The president of
Russia must stand tall and eminent.’" What would be
your comment on this passage?

{[Shumeyko] I don’t want to insult anybody. It all
depends on who judges height. Maybe I do stand tall so
far as some people are concerned.

That is not the point. Liking yourself is one thing. Stupid
people like themselves. Being self-confident — sure. I
am self-confident, I tell you that quite firmly. I know
my worth. And I am marching through life with utter
confidence. [Shumeyko ends]

In Place of an Afterword

Knowing Vladimir Filippovich as a cheerful and
straightforward person, we asked him finally to tell us
a joke.

{[Shumeyko] Two peasants are walking along and sud-
denly fall into a pit. A deep pit. One says: "What are
we going to do?" The other replies: "I know! You just
sit there quietly.”

Here is another joke. The dragon Gorynych attacked
a village and burned it to the ground The people go
running to Ilya Muromets: “Help us, Ilyusha!" “Is it
a big dragon?” “Huge, it covers half the sky. It’s
black and terrifying and it’s stealing the women!” "I'll
have to have a think, guys." "How long will that take,
Ilyushenka?” "A week." "But in a week it will have
killed us all!”

They go running off to see Dobryna Nikitich. They tell
him the whole story, and he asks: "Have you been to
see Ilya?" "Yes." “What did Ilya say?” “He would have
a think." “How long for?" "A week.” "I’m going to have
a think, too." "For a week?" "For three days." "But that
is too long again!" So they run off to Alesha Popovich:
“Dear Alesha, you are the only protector we have left!"
And they tell him everything. Alesha shouts: “Bring
me my steed! And my armor! Come here, my sword-
bearer!" The peasants fall to their knees: "Alesha! Aren't
you going to have a think?" "What's the point? I’ve got
to run away!"

Duma Chairman Rybkin Says Russia Needs ‘Labor
Party’

MM1110185895 Moscow ROSSIYSKAYA GAZETA

in Russian 12 Oct 95 pp 1-2

{Interview with State Duma Speaker Ivan Rybkin
by Mikhail Gurevich; place and date not given:
“Bloc Emerges from Blockade" — first paragraph is
ROSSIYSKAYA GAZETA introduction}

(FBIS Translated Text] It is wrong for Ivan Rybkin’s
center-left bloc to complain that it is not being paid at-
tention. Virtually every week some observer produces
a gloomy forecast regarding its future. But the rumors
of its death have as always proved exaggerated. State
Duma Chairman Ivan Rybkin, the bloc’s leader, answers
your ROSSIYSKAYA GAZETA correspondent’s ques-
tions.

[Gurevich] Has Rybkin’s bloc proved viable as an
election formation and group of confederates? What will
be the main item on its program?

[Rybkin]} I can now say clearly that the bloc has proved
viable. Its formation was democratic. People came
along, studied the program, the leader's opinion, and
one another’s views. Some left, and that is their right.
But over 50 movements, associations, trade unions, and
associations of various kinds stayed. I can say that the
bloc is working successfully in this difficult election
campaign. And I am convinced that we are just bound
to get through the 5-percent barrier. We have the support
of agrarians, trade unions, entrepreneurs, industrialists,
bankers, and, most importantly, voters.

In our program we have singled out five principles: so-
cial justice, order, citizens’ personal security, construc-
tion, and realism. Our program is different from the
declarations made by other parties, association, blocs,
bloclets, and cells in that for the last few years my col-
leagues and I have been implementing firmly and con-
sistently the ideas that we have conceived. We will cer-
tainly carry out our current plans. The bloc comprises
people who have real action to their credit over many

FBIS-SOV-95-198
13 October 1995

years, people with tremendous experience of governing
regions large and small and collectives — engaged in
production, science, higher education, culture, and state
organs of power. There is a mixture of age groups: the
wisdom and experience of the old; middle-aged people
with a mature approach who still have a good bit of
life left in them; and young, thrusting, energetic peo-
ple. The bloc includes the Russian Youth League —
the largest youth organization. Little attention is now
being devoted to young people; everything has been let
slip. The elderly and middle-aged are indebted to young
people since they have helped us in the past.

[Gurevich] Whom do you see as the bloc’s partner?

{Rybkin} Our partners are people whom we know,
those with whom we cooperate and have long-standing
relations: namely, the Agrarian Party, "Women of
Russia," and many social democratic associations that
are taking part in the elections. God knows what kind
of success they will have, whether they will collect
the required 200,000 signatures. Our bloc has collected
nearly 250,000, and quickly at that.

[Gurevich] There is no authority without a strong party.
Do you not want to set up your own party — not an
electoral bloc, but a party? If you do, what will you call
it? And what are your personal plans?

{Rybkin] I think that there should be a party. Right now
I would be wary of giving it a name in order to prevent
the name turning up later somewhere else. Although
Yuriy Vladimirovich Petrov, my colleague in the bloc,
and I have been discussing this since 1991. There are
like-minded people and painstaking, unhurried work is
under way. A labor party is needed to represent the
working man and defend his rights.

I am standing in a single-seat district — I was nominated
by my fellow inhabitants of Voronezh, who have known
me since I was a babe in arms. In all 9,000 signatures
were collected in three days — how can the bloc be
weak? I had 16 rivals. I said to myself: "I must stand
and I must win."

If I see that there are intelligent, practical people who
can go further and have a broader outlook, undoubted
talent, and all the qualities required to be chairman of
the Duma, I will definitely give up my post; but if I do
not, you understand what I am driving at.

[Gurevich] The last session of Russia’s Fifth State
Duma is under way. What issues and draft laws will
you be putting first?

[Rybkin} Priority is being given to all the social laws.
Needless to say, we will try to ratify the draft 1996

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-95-198
13 October 1995

budget. We must not leave it until next year, for the
next Duma.

[Gurevich] A few days ago a session of the Russian Fed-
eration Presidential Council for Local Self-Government
was held. You and the prime minister, who is also the
leader of an electoral association — “Russia Is Our
Home” — cochair the council. Do you see local self-
government as the basis of a revived authority today?

[Rybkin} This is a key question for the country now.
There will not be a strong center or a strong unified
Russia without strong, autonomous regions. Everything
cannot be seen from Moscow, and the regions need to
be given the maximum autonomy. At the same time the
federal center must be concerned with pursuing a unified
policy for the whole state. The Federation Council must
be formed immediately 13 December and comprise the
regional elite: the heads of legislative assemblies and
local administrations. It must coordinate interests and
decide which powers of authority should be given to
the center and which should be left at local level. Then
every local administrator must realize that he cannot see
everything from his capital either — whether it be the
capital of a republic, a kray, or an oblast center. That is
when local self-government structures start to emerge.

It is my profound conviction that some local self-
government should have state support — both in the
federal budget and in the regional budget. The rest will
come from the local self-government unit itself and self-
funding.

The council session discussed at length how to make
self- t better and more effective. People’s
creativity must not be curbed in all its endeavors;
initiative must be unleashed. It is necessary for people
to co and accept the ideas of reform and the
ideas of local self-government.

Needless to say, it is not all that easy. But Tyutchev
said that Russia has one ally, namely history. And for
centuries history has resolved all the ordeals that have
beset Russia in its favor. That will happen this time too.

Skokov Interviewed on KRO, Lebed, Yeltsin

MK1010064795 Moscow MOSKOVSKIYE NOVOSTI
in Russian No. 67, 1-8 Oct 95 p 8

{Interview with Yuriy Skokov by Aleksandr Zhilin and
Sergey Yastrebov: "I Have Never Enjoyed the Support
of Influential Patrons")}

(FBIS Translated Text] There have been many rumors
about Yuriy Skokov, leader of the Congress of Russian
Communities [KRO], making a comeback soon to a
position of power as prime minister. MOSKOVSKIYE

DUMA ELECTION ISSUES 21

NOVOSTI [MN] correspondents asked the KRO leader
himself for comments on these rumors.

[Skokov] All these rumors are absolutely groundless.
I did not meet with the president, and we had no
discussions whatsoever on my appointment as prime
minister despite what is being said today on television
and written in the papers. In a word, as far as I
am concerned, no change of position is in the offing.
Whether the president will continue to work with
Chernomyrdin or whether he appoints someone else
is the president’s constitutional right. What I cannot
understand is why my name should have been bandied
about of late absolutely without any grounds. Yes,
there are indeed serious differences between myself
and Chernomyrdin; and yes, I indeed disagree with the
current government's policies, which I openly declare.

[MN] That is to say, you do not intend to link your
political future with the present authorities. If the KRO
wins the elections, in what role do you see yourself?

[Skokov] Today I am working so as to conduct the
election campaign in an organized and sensible manner
and to secure the greatest possible number of votes and
seats in parliament. After the Duma elections it will
be possible to speak of the KRO’s political prospects
and its leaders, while now it is not serious to let
one’s imagination get carried away on this subject.
Going into the elections, we are putting forward two
fundamental tasks connected with our country’s national
interests. First, we all have to survive under these
difficult conditions. Second, to protect Russia as a single
state. I am not worried what position I will hold after
solving this two-pronged task.

{(MN] Your record of service indicates that you have
never emerged in a top position in big-time politics,
remaining in secondary roles. You invited Aleksandr
Lebed into the KRO. Does this mean you are prepared
to once again retire into the background?

[Skokov] The notion of “first-second" in politics is
conventional. If a team is playing on the political field,
the important thing is to benefit from the strong points
of each player. I worked in Boris Yeltsin’s team and
saw the way the people idolized him at that time, and
my task consisted of helping the president, and I was
not worried about being first or second.

My relationship with Aleksandr Lebed is somewhat
different. We are partners who are working together to
create a team capable of solving the tasks I spoke of
earlier. If we manage it, this will be our shared victory,
and it is absolutely unimportant who of us is first and
who is second. We have no differences in this respect.
I value Aleksandr Ivanovich for his decency, natural

Need for CIS Security System Viewed

MMI1110150395 Moscow KRASNAYA ZVEZDA
in Russian 7 Oct 95 p 3

[Article by Colonel Nikolay Khomchenko and Major
Igor Bizyuk of the Russian Federation Armed Forces
General Staff: “All Is Not Yet Lost; Polemical Notes
on Some Aspects of Military Cooperation Within the
CIS Framework"]

{[FBIS Translated Text] The further the time of the
CIS’ disappearance from the world map recedes from
us the more clearly there emerges in the consciousness
of people living in the post-Soviet space the thought
that we have all lost out. After all, it is no secret that
the former union republics are experiencing increasing
difficulties both in establishing their statehood and
in developing political, economic, military, and other
relations within the CIS. Considering the specific nature
of the newspaper, let us dwell in more detail on the
military aspect of these relations.

Questions of military-political cooperation within the
CIS framework, as we know, have been reflected in
the collective security concept approved in February
1995. But today that is no longer enough because
alongside the integration processes in Europe there
is also an intensification of the military threat there.
It is not out of the question that we could become
witnesses to the emergence of a new world order
(the expansion of NATO's borders to the East) which
will be more dangerous than the one which existed
previously. Is it not time under these conditions to
move from slogans and promises to each other to real
deeds — to the creation of an effective mechanism for
collaboration between states which actually complies
with the collective security treaty? Historical experience
shows us that however powerful a state may be it
cannot safeguard its security alone — for that it must
have strategic allies. Is this problem resolved by the
existing “Partnership for Peace" program? Hardly, since
it does not offer equal cooperation and consequently
cannot fully safeguard the interests of Russia and the
other CIS countries (and ultimately their security). In
our view the most acceptable thing in this case is to
seek to safeguard our own security in conjunction with
our existing “genetic allies" who are the majority of
republics of the former Soviet Union.

Problems exist here too. But many of them are artificial
and are of a subjective nature, proceeding from specific
forces which in their majority are pursuing populist aims
and also from the imperfect legal norm base. We can
be convinced of this if only from the example of the
operational equipping of the CIS countries’ territory. It
seems to us that it is here that all the prerequisites ex-

INTERREGIONAL AFFAIRS 23

ist for integration. The existing military infrastructure
bequeathed to the countries from the USSR and in its
majority of the same type and locked into a single sys-
tem combined with the Russian infrastructure would in
general terms make it possible to safeguard those coun-
tries’ security. To proceed along the path of accession to
NATO would essentially mean totally restructuring the
existing system of military infrastructure installations
(since such an alliance presupposes the standardization
of weapons, military equipment, and all servicing sys-
tems). And that would mean rejecting the approaches
we have formed toward the development of military-
technical thinking (the so-called military-technical pol-
icy), enormous financial expenses, and the loss of jobs
in the military-industrial complex. Moreover, there are
no guarantees that this alliance would indeed fully safe-
guard our interests.

During the time of the CIS’ existence many of its
members have become convinced of how hard it is to
implement their own independent policy and safeguard
and protect their own interests in the world community
without having an adequately reliable guarantor of their
own security in the shape of the armed forces. In the
absence of a firm economic base and the necessary
experience in building a national army it is hard not only
to create armed forces meeting modern requirements but
also to ensure the maintenance and retention of the little
that is still left since the division of the USSR Armed
Forces (weapons, military infrastructure, and so forth).
Subsequently far larger funds for restoring, for instance,
the necessary military infrastructure installations will be
required than now. So their conservation, development,
and improvement are possible only by joint efforts.
Naturally the approach toward this issue should be
mutually advantageous and interdependent for all CIS
countries so that it is possible to safeguard the security
both of all members of this alliance and of each of them.

Here we can cite the experience of constructing, mod-
ernizing, and making joint use of military infrastruc-
ture installations by the states which used to belong to
the Warsaw Pact. In the Warsaw Pact countries, as we
know, this question was closely linked with the USSR
Armed Forces groupings deployed on their territories.
The plan for operational equipping of the territories of
these countries provided for bilateral agreements be-
tween the Warsaw Pact states for the creation of military
infrastructure installations since it was proposed to use
these states’ armed forces in conjunction with the group-
ings of Soviet troops there. On the territories of these
states a well developed network of military infrastruc-
ture installations was created in some places jointly, in
some places only with the Soviet Union's funds, backing
up both the troops’ daily activity and the implementa-

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

2 INTERREGIONAL AFFAIRS

tion of operations, including strategic operations, by the
joint groupings. The Warsaw Pact countries’ military in-
frastructure largely accorded with the approaches of the
Soviet Union the only differeuce being that it was pro-
posed to use its installations primarily in the interests of
the groupings of Soviet troops which were located there
and were fulfilling a collective task. The withdrawal of
the Soviet (Russian) troops from East Europe has shown
that at that period the question of financing these mea-
sures was not specified and agreed. As a result of which
we were obliged, when the troops were withdrawn, to
leave behind most of the installations of our own or the
joint infrastructure, receiving nothing in return at home
(apart from some housing for the families of service-
men).

Considering this experience within the framework of
collective security, in our view it is essential to deter-
mine in advance the solution of a number of priority
tasks concerning the operational equipping of the terri-
tory.

First of all in elaborating agreements on the joint use,
development, and improvement of the military infra-
structure the contracting sides must speak "the same
language." That is it is essential to have a document
(memorandum) which would determine common con-
cepts, provisions, and terminology in the field of the
operational equipping of the country’s territory and not
allow them to be interpreted arbitrarily. Thus it is a case
of creating a unified legal norm base for the CIS coun-
tries which would include initially at least a minimum
list of information concerning this issue. The mecha-
nism and procedure for compiling this list could be de-
termined on the basis of the elaboration of unified views
of this issue.

The need also arises to introduce the relevant changes
and additions to legal norm acts of the CIS states al-
ready in operation concerning questions of the mutual
use of military infrastructure establishments and the de-
ployment of armed forces on the territory of other states.
As we know, the constitutions of a number of CIS coun-
tries (Belarus, Moldova) rule out the presence of foreign
states’ armed forces on their territories and the location
of foreign bases and other military infrastructure estab-
lishments. Naturally difficulties could arise when these
states’ legislative organs ratify agreements with other
countries (including Russia) on the creation of a unified
system of military infrastructure installations.

That is a question for the legislative authorities. At the
same time we must realize that a collective security
treaty can hardly be considered effective unless it
provides for the joint use of the military infrastructure.
We must also determine the legal status of military

FBIS-SOV-95-198
13 October 1995

infrastructure installations which could be used in
the interests of collective security: Either they will
be national installations made available for use by
other CIS countries or they will be installations which
are jointly created on the basis of the proportional
participation of each country. And perhaps on the
basis of the most important and complex military
installations (for instance special strategic nuclear forces
installations, ABM, aerospace defense system, and other
installations) situated on the territory of other CIS
countries it would be expedient to create Russian
military bases which would function in the interests
of all CIS countries. In any case the procedure for
creating and using military infrastructure installations
should be determined within the framework of the
collective security treaty (as an appendix to it) or on
the basis of bilateral (multilateral) treaties between the
CIS countries. International experience in the creation
and joint use of military infrastructure installations is
probably also of interest here. For instance, the status
of U.S. bases and installations abroad is regulated
by many bilateral and multilateral agreements and
accords, to wit — treaties on the lease of a territory,
treaties and agreements on mutual security and defense,
agreements on the location of military bases and military
infrastructure installations between a military-political
alliance and its individual members...

In our view multilateral (collective) treaties and agree-
ments on mutual security and defense signed within the
NATO framework merit particular attention. They de-
termine primarily the alliance’s collective tasks: the pro-
cedure for deploying troops on foreign territory, for the
use of bases, military infrastructure installations, sectors
of terrain, and responsibility for ensuring the defense
of a particular NATO zone, and others. For the suc-
cessful implementation of these tasks an agreement is
signed between the countries specifically involved (as
a rule this agreement is between two states at govern-
ment level) which also stipulates the specific details of
their joint activity. As a result the agreements signed by
the countries belonging to the same alliance, bilateral in
form and collective in content, are the implementation
of the bloc’s common plans and tasks. As we can see
the range of cooperation in the mutual use of military
infrastructure installations is very wide. But agreements
in the field of the operational equipping of territory are
not enough on their own. For their practical implemen-
tation, as we can see from the proposed scheme [the
article is printed around a diagram showing the vari-
ous subdivisions of the CIS states’ collective security
council] the relevant structures in the organs of military
management of the states united by the collective se-
curity treaty are required. In our opinion the following
tasks should be assigned to these structures:

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-95-198
13 October 1995

— the leadership of the carving up of the geostrategic
space of the territory of the treaty’s signatory states;

— the preparation of recommendations for the leader-
ship regarding the determination of strategic orienta-
tions;

— the organization of collaboration with general (main)
staffs of the CIS states’ armed forces for the joint use,
construction, and modernization of military infrastruc-
ture installations.

This work should obviously be based on the planning
of measures for the operational equipping of their
territories in national interests and in the interests of
the security of the entire CIS within the framework of
the joint program for the development of the military
infrastructure.

The next, no less important, task is the restoration of
the single strategic space of the CIS states which are
signatories of the collective security treaty. The solu-
tion of this global task is connected primarily with the
conservation and now the restoration of military infra-
structure installations ensuring warning against a mis-
sile attack, the management of strategic and operational
intelligence, and the implementation of space and air
defense.

The first steps in this direction have been taken — doc-
uments have been signed on the creation of a unified air
defense system (including the infrastructure) for the CIS
countries. It has turned out that many states cannot inde-
pendently cover the air space above their territory (vast
regions have been outside radar observation and con-
trol). Approximately the same situation is taking place
in the system of communications, hydronavigation, and
the transport complex, which also requires the creation
of similar systems within the CIS framework.

There is another path — the creation of duplicate mil-
itary infrastructure installations on their own territories
to replace those left on the territories of other CIS coun-
tries. But right now that is a difficult task to perform.

And one last point. We cannot view the collective
security system of the CIS countries as a counterweight
to the NATO bloc that exists at present. At the same
time this is not an “elite club for selected states." Here,
we think, there will be room for all those who want to
join, including those from East Europe because, apart
from geopolitical proximity and interdependence, these
CIS states and countries are linked by over 50 years’
experience of their inte” sation. And as of today there are
still enough points o{ ~satact for fruitful cooperation on
fundamentally new and mutually advantageous terms,
including in the military field.

Moscow Favors World’s ‘Impartial Approach’ to
Croatia

LD1210203395 Moscow INTERFAX in English
1914 GMT 12 Oct 95

(From the "Diplomatic Panorama” feature)

(FBIS Transcribed Text] MOSCOW, Oct 12 (INTER-
FAX) — The Russian Foreign Ministry’s spokesman
Grigoriy Karasin said Russia favored a well-thought-out
and impartial approach of the world community towards
all illegal actions committed in the former Yugoslavia
“no matter which party to the conflict has launched
them."

Karasin praised the statement made by the chairman
of the U.N. Security Council on October 3. The U.N.
Security Council’s head said he was seriously concerned
about the actions of the Croatian authorities towards
refugees staying in Croatia, towards “both the Serbs
expelled from Krajina and the Moslems threatened with
an expel to Bosnia."

Karasin repeated that Russia had proposed the draft
of this statement to the U.N. Security Council on
September 29.

Karasin said that the document "highlights the inadmis-
sibility of returning refugees by force to the areas which
they have left." He said the document underlined that
such actions ran counter to the Geneva Convention of
1951 which had also been signed by Croatia.

Karasin said the U.N. Security Council’s statement
“demands that the Croatian government put an end to all
actions violating human rights and respect the rights of
the local Serbian population." He said the U.N. security
Council “urges Zagrev to continue providing shelter to
all refugees regardless of their ethnic affiliation and to
remove all restrictions concerning the time for the return

of refugees.”

bilateral cooperation were formulated in a message from

del Castro the day before. “Both states have chosen the
only correct path in their mutual relations, aimed at in-
tegration into the world economic system,” he stressed.

Russia is not reckoning, he noted, on cooperation
with Cuba providing immediate "crazy profit.” Oleg
Soskovets sees the main task today as that of seeking
mechanisms and possibilities for bilateral cooperation.

traditions," he stressed. The first deputy prime minister
proposed to the Cuban side that the possibility of setting
up joint structures, banking structures among them,
should be examined. A Russian-Cuban joint enterprise is
already to be set up at the end of this year in Cienfuegos
on the basis of the oil-refining combine, and a similar
project is to be implemented in Santiago de Cuba at the
beginning of next year.

At the talks, the Cuban minister told the Russian dele-
gation about the situation on the island, having stressed
the great economic and financial difficulties. In future,
when Cuba will be able to restore its economic potential,
it will be possible "to talk about cancelling debts,” he
explained. The minister stressed the importance of the

Meets Cuban Armed Forces Minister Castro

LD1310063595 Moscow ITAR-TASS World Service
in Russian 2310 GMT 12 Oct 95

[By ITAR-TASS special correspondents Oleg Velichko
and Konstantin Zhukovskiy]

(FBIS Translated Text] Havana, 13 Oct (ITAR-TASS)
— Oleg Soskovets, first deputy chairman of the Russian
Federation government, who is here on an official visit,
met Raul Castro, minister of the Revolutionary Armed
Forces of Cuba, on Thursday.

During the meeting, the sides discussed in detail issues
relating to Russian-Cuban cooperation in the military
sphere. The subject of the Russian radioelectronics
center situated not far from Havana occupied a special
place in the talks. The problem of financing this facility
in the coming year was examined in particular.

According to information received by ITAR-TASS
from a source in the Russian delegation, Moscow
intends to offer Havana a draft agreement on setting
up joint enterprises in the military sphere, including
a specialized center for repairing special equipment.
To all appearances, the sides are limiting themselves
solely to discussion of the Russian proposals within
the framework of the first deputy prime minister’s visit,
and the military departments of both countries will be
engaged directly in signing them.

Cuba Visit Said To Shift Foreign Policy

MK1310091295 Moscow NEZAVISIMAYA GAZETA
in Russian 12 Oct 95 pl

[Report by Aleksandr Zhelenin under the "Visit" rubric:
"Soskovets in Cuba. A New Turnabout in Relations
With the Island of Freedom"]

{[FBIS Translated Text] To all appearances, the five-day
Official visit to Cuba by First Deputy Prime Minister
Oleg Soskovets, which began on 10 October, will not
only be a new milestone in relations between the two
countries, but will also become an integral part of the
process of revising Russia’s foreign policy doctrine.

Such a conclusion can be drawn if only from the visit’s
Stated objectives, such as the signing of a protocol on
trade turnover and payments for 1996-1997, which calls
for Russian oil deliveries in exchange for sugar, and
a memorandum on cooperation in the peaceful use of
nuclear power. Talks will also be held with the Cuban
leadership on cooperation in the production and refining
of oil and gas, and also on the nickel industry. Therefore,

FBIS-SOV-95-198
13 October 1995

the issue is thawing and restoring, virtually to the full
extent, former economic — and not only economic —
relations.

This is attributable first of all to purely practical reasons:
Since sugar production in Russia and Ukraine is on
the decline, it should be imported, cheaply if possible.
Evidently, buying sugar from Cuba is still profitable,
even if one has to pay for it with oil.

But the most sensational news concerns the resumption
of military-technological cooperation with the Island
of Freedom. If this really happens, one can state that
in its bid to restore relations with its former allies,
Russia is beginning to act proceeding from its own
interests. During the Soviet era cooperation with Cuba
was mandatory for the USSR for ideological reasons,
just as after 1991 precisely the same reasons caused a
drastic scaling down of ties. Ideological premises have
changed, but not so the approach.

Soskovets’ visit may mark the adoption of Russia’s first
new role since 1991, which manifests itself in a much
more independent policy on the international scene —
in deed, not just in words.

It is quite possible that the resumption of military co-
operation between Russia and Cuba has been prompted
by the plans for NATO's eastward expansion. In this re-
gard, the Russian move to meet Cuba half way appears
to be fully logical.

Karasin on Border Guards’ Attacked in Tajikistan

LD1210151195 Moscow ITAR-TASS in English
1412 GMT 12 Oct 95

(By Yuriy Kozlov]

(FBIS Transcribed Text) MOSCOW, October 12
(ITAR-TASS) — "The Russian Foreign Ministry as-
sesses the attack on Russian borderguards in Tajikistan
as a planned criminal action, and puts all the blame
for it on the leadership of the Tajik Islamic Revival
Movement (IRM)," ITAR-TASS was told today by
Head of the Foreign Ministry’s Information and Press
Directorate Grigoriy Karasin.

As earlier reported, a vehicle, carrying servicemen from
the group of Russian borderguards in Tajikistan, was
fired upon on October 10. Seven men, including four
Russians and three Tajiks, were killed. Four other
servicemen were wounded. The fire from small arms
and heavy weapons was opened by a group of IRM
men, commanded by Said Afzali.

The official spokesman of the Russian Foreign Ministry
drew attention to the provocative nature of the incident,

opposition is resorting, is to “make the Russian border
