Chubays: Economic Growth Will Begin in 1996

LD1210095495 Moscow ITAR-TASS in English
0850 GMT 12 Oct 95

[By Andrey Loshchilin]

(FBIS Transcribed Text] WASHINGTON, October 12
(ITAR-TASS) — Reporting to the annual meeting of
the Boards of Governors of the International Monetary
Fund and the World Bank, Russian First Vice-Premier
Anatoliy Chubays pointed out that the Russian Federal
Budget’s deficit equalled to about three per cent of
the gross domestic product in January-September as
compared to the corresponding period of 1994. He
noted that “all the set tasks are on the whole being
implemented". The vice-premier’s report was issued
here as an official document of the meeting.

"The amount of official currency reserves has more
than doubled since the beginning of the year. The
introduction of the currency exchange band guaranteed
the rouble’s relative stability," the document says. "The
production slump slowed down simultaneously with
this, and economic growth was recorded in some sectors
of the Russian industry, primarily in its exporting
branches. The drop of the gross domestic product
equalled to four per cent during the first nine months
of this year as compared to 16 per cent last year. We
expect the country’s economy to grow next year, for the
first time since the beginning of the reforms in Russia."

Pre-Election Concessions Swell Money Supply

MM1210142795 Moscow IZVESTIYA in Russian
12 Oct 95 p2

[Article by Irina Savvateyeva: "Inflation May Be Falling
but More and More Money Is Being Pumped into the
Economy"]}

(FBIS Translated Text] Despite the recent record low
inflation figures (in the first week of October prices rose
by just 0.8 percent) October brought with it a note of
alarm over the stability of the monetary and financial
situation in the country.

The point is that the first highly unpleasant symptom
surfaced this month: One of the main indicators of the
government and Central Bank monetary program which
records the growth in the money supply — the so-called
Central Bank credit to broad government [rasshirennoye
pravitelstvo] — exceeded its target. In other words,
budget funding targets at all levels have been exceeded.

The “overspend” is not all that great at the moment:
300 billion rubles [R]. And according to Economics
Minister Ye. Yasin, there is no cause for panic — the
problem can be resolved in the normal course of events.

FBIS-SOV-95-198
13 October 1995

Nevertheless, the question of whether the "overspend"
was a one-off or the beginning of a new trend is highly
topical.

For example, it was suddenly discovered in October
that the local organs of power sharply increased their
spending. Is this a trend created by a rise in the
cost of electricity, tariffs, and municipal services or a
coincidence?

Oleg Vyugin, director of the Finance Ministry’s depart-
ment of macroeconomics, was in no hurry with conclu-
sions, noting that the ministry’s statistics need careful
study and verification.

Meanwhile, Sergey Vasilyev, deputy economics minis-
ter, is more anxious about the situation in general. He
believes that the R300 billion which “unexpectedly” ap-
peared is the perfectly logical consequence of the situa-
tion in the country: The government has "weakened" in
the face of very strong pre-election pressure.

In the deputy minister’s opinion, what we are seeing
first and foremost here are non-budget funds, grain
purchases, and the need to fund the Army’s food supply,
the cost of which was not included in the 1995 budget.
And of course Chechnya.

The overspend in terms of non-budget funds is primarily
due to the situation affecting the pension fund, where
actual revenue is falling catastrophically. On the one
hand this is a consequence of the sharp decline in the
proportion of wages in the population’s incomes which
has been noticed recently. This decline in turn is leading
to a significant reduction in the tax base — the source
of revenue for the pension fund.

As for grain purchases, the state is actually having to
fund them once again. The situation in this area is as
follows. This year the countryside’s debt was dealt with
by means of bills of exchange. The assumption was that
these bills of exchange would be sold in the market. But
under pressure from the agrarian lobby, which has made
skillful use of the election situation, what has happened
essentially is that another credit line has been opened up
for farmers. Generally speaking the sources of finance
that the government still has for plugging the various
gaps are certainly not without their down side. The
Central Bank can buy state securities and gold from the
Finance Ministry. The latter can also sell hard currency
to the Central Bank, among others.

"Essentially we are talking about additional money
supply," Sergey Vasilyev notes. "If the pre-election
attack on the government is sustained and this kind
of funding is not halted," the deputy minister stressed,
"it could all end fairly badly: The monetary program
will disintegrate before our eyes, Russia will have great

is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-95-198
13 October 1995

problems obtaining credits from international financial
organizations, and generally speaking a country which
has grown accustomed to an average inflation rate of 3
percent a month will find it quite difficult adapting to a
7 percent rate."

EBRD Provides $303 Million for Small Business
Loans

MM1210133895 Moscow IZVESTIYA (Financial
Section) in Russian 12 Oct 95 pl

[Report by Vladimir Kovalevskiy: "Western Loans
Arrive in Moscow. But Only for Small Enterprises"]

{FBIS Translated Text] The European Bank for Recon-
struction and Development [EBRD] has begun provid-
ing small business loans in Moscow. This project is be-
ing implemented in conjunction with the Russian Fed-
eration Savings Bank. The EBRD is allocating a total
of $303 million.

The EBRD has been providing support for small busi-
ness in various parts of Russia for 18 months now. In
this time around 1,000 loans have been issued. They are
being provided in rubles in amounts not in excess of
$20,000, at 140-150 percent annual interest. According
to EBRD spokesperson Anna Peters-Freitag, the scheme
has proven its viability in Russia — there have been vir-
tually no defaulters. During the first phase, loans will
be issued in Moscow via a branch of the Savings Bank.

The main condition for the provision of credit is that the
client must be solvent. His financial solvency is not only
checked out on the basis of documentation submitted
(the enterprise balance sheet, for instance), as is usual in
our country. Experts especially recruited to the Savings
Bank staff visit the borrower’s home without fail, study
his family and financial status. Anna Peters-Freitag
thinks that these studies make it possible to obtair
an objective idea of the borrower’s entrepreneurial
capabilities and potential and to be certain that the loan
will be put to use and will be repaid.

Similar loan schemes have been operating in countries
with high inflation for about 15 years now. The EBRD
has now extended a hand to Russia’s small enterprises.
World experience shows that recovery from crisis and
the rebuilding of the economy always occur via the
intensive development of small business. In Japan and
Germany, for instance, small enterprises of this kind
now account for around 80 percent of all jobs.

RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS

Central Bank Money, Credit Policy Examined

MM1210132595 Moscow IZVESTIYA (Financial
Section) in Russian 12 Oct 95 p 1,2

[Article by Yevgeniya Pismennaya: “Bank of Russia
Promises To Fight Inflation Without Forgetting Interests
of Economy as a Whole")

(FBIS Translated Text] The Central Bank has elaborated
the draft basic guidelines for monetary and credit
policy for next year. When it was forming them the
Central Bank proceeded from the need to further reduce
inflation. The slowing down of inflation will lead to a
reduction in inflation expectations and will allow real
interest rates on deposits to reach positive amounts.

The Central Bank rejects direct credit for the federal
budget deficit and the granting of centralized credits to
economic sectors. The envisaged budget deficit should
be financed from noninflationary sources — through
loans on the market.

One of the Central Bank’s main tasks next year is
to curb the growth of the money mass in circulation.
For that it is essential to increase the reliability of the
banking system and to increase citizens’ confidence in
it.

The Central Bank's currency policy provides for two
main goals. The dynamics of the currency exchange
rate must be brought into line with the development
of inflation and the dynamic of the main components
of the money mass. It is essential to ensure a currency
exchange rate regime which complies with the require-
ments of monetary and credit regulation and the main-
tenance of a balanced balance of payments and a high
level of liquidity for the Russian Federation to ensure
payments on its international commitments and the sta-
ble functioning of the system of foreign economic rela-
tions.

The Central Bank will regulate market fluctuations
in the ruble’s nominal exchange rate. The policy of
consolidating currency control over export and import
operations and the full return of the currency profit from
exports to accounts in fully empowered Russian banks
will continue.

The Bank of Russia plans to improve the system for
monitoring the country’s currency market, extending it
to the short-term currency market. The bank intends to
smooth over and equalize exchange rate fluctuations on
various segments of the currency market and its regional
and institutional sectors.

In determining monetary guidelines for the end of 1996
the Central Bank has proceeded from the premise that
with a substantial reduction in inflation the trend toward

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

4 RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS

the slowing down of the production slump will persist
or this slump will be successfully halted. So restrictive
monetary policy principles must be retained. But at the
same time in the Central Bank’s opinion conditions are
already ripe for an increase in the money mass in real
terms.

But because of the uncertainty in the development of
some characteristics of the monetary sphere (like the
degree of dollarization of the economy and the structure
of the money mass) the bank cannot unequivocally
predict the dynamic of the turnover velocity. So an
alternative approach toward the elaboration of monetary
and credit policy is topical now.

The Central Bank has elaborated three options for
guidelines for a unified state monetary and credit policy
for the end of 1996 based on two scenarios. Scenario A
proceeds from qualitative changes in the formation of
the money supply. Scenario B includes existing trends
in monetary and credit policy.

Operational procedure for the Central Bank’s monetary
and credit policy will be based on monitoring of target
indicators for the money base in the broad sense (cash
in circulation and in the commercial banks, assets in
the compulsory reserves fund, and reserves in banks’

correspondent accounts).

The Bank of Russia will support the stability of interest
rates in the economy. In its short-term policy the Central
Bank will consider changes within the year and changes
within the month in the banking system’s demand for
reserves (liquid assets), helping to smooth out excessive
fluctuations in interest rates on the money market.

Next year as in the past the Central Bank will not grant
new direct centralized credits to commercial banks and
re-form existing debts on centralized credits and interest
on them.

The Ministry of Finances in conjunction with the
Central Bank considers it necessary to examine the
question of transferring in 1996 to the state internal
debt under the guarantee of the organs of executive
power of the components of the Federation the debts
to commercial banks as of 1 January next year owed on
centralized credits and interest on them by enterprises of
the fuel and energy complex and other sectors totaling
about 1 trillion rubles [R], extinguishing these debts
over the next 10 years in equal shares. These debts can
be included in the Russian Federation state internal debt
within the framework of the upper limit set for 1996 for
the state debt through the Ministry of Finances’ issue
of promissory notes to the Central Bank to be paid off
in 10 years as of 1998 in equal shares with an interest
rate of 10 percent per annum.

FBIS-SOV-95-198
13 October 1995

Alongside the placement of credit resources through
credit auctions which have been held every month since
January 1994, the Central Bank intends to introduce
methods of refinancing commercial banks like Lombard
credits and the second discounting of promissory notes.

It is also planned to introduce a new instrument for re-
financing commercial banks — operations of the REPO
type on the open market (the redeemable contract) with
the use of state securities issued on market terms.

The Central Bank is examining the question of granting
centralized credits to reliable commercial banks experi-
encing temporary financial difficulties.

To regulate the volumes of money in circulation the
Central Bank intends to expand the instruments of
monetary and credit policy by carrying out operations
to attract the funds of commercial banks into deposit
accounts on a contract, paid basis at a rate lower than
the interbank deposit rate. At the same time the Central
Bank ensures the 100 percent guarantee of the prompt
return of deposits and the interest on them.

The Bank of Russia intends to establish a single
rate for all assets attracted (in national currency) for
compulsory reserves as a percentage of the credit
organization’s liabilities (compulsory reserves norm).
This will simplify the procedure for accounting and will
make it possible to determine more objectively the size
of the sum of compulsory reserves.

It is proposed to reduce the period of regulation of
compulsory reserves and to make it the same and
compulsory for all commercial banks. This will prepare
conditions for the gradual transition to a daily regulation
of compulsory reserves.

To establish a single methodology for compulsory re-
serves it is planned to transfer institutions of the Savings
Bank to a common procedure for depositing assets in
the compulsory reserves fund. Here it is planned to ex-
pand the base for the formation of compulsory reserves
of the Savings Bank institutions by transferring to the
reservation of all funds attracted and not only the in-
crease in the population’s investments.

With a view to increasing the commercial banks’
liability for observing reserve requires the law “On
the Central Bank of the Russian Federation (Bank of
Russia)" adopted 26 June 1995 provides for tougher
sanctions for the delayed and incomplete deposit of
the assets attracted in the compulsory reserves fund.
The Bank of Russia has been granted the right to the
undisputed levying of unpaid sums and also the use
of fines within the established limits but at not more
than double the rate for refinancing. The procedure for
implementing these rights will be set out in the provision

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-95-198
13 October 1995

which is being elaborated on compulsory reserves for
credit organizations deposited in the Central Bank.

Next year the Bank of Russia in implementing mon-
etary and credit policy intends to continue to use the
mechanism of regulation that is the interest rate on its
operations. It is planned to maintain the rate for refi-
nancing commercial banks and the rates for the second
discounting of promissory notes and for Lombard credit

RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS 55

at the level of existing quoted interbank rates designed
for credits with comparable repayment deadlines or at
a level in excess of these rates.

In establishing the rate for refinancing the Central
Bank intends also to consider the influence of interest
policy on the formation of expenses on production and
consequently the inflation of costs.

International Affairs
CE President Views Organization’s Principles

AU1210151495 Kiev HOLOS UKRAYINY
in Ukrainian 7 Oct 95 pp 6,7

{"Exclusive interview" with Miguel Angel Martinez,
president of the Council of Europe Parliamentary
Assembly, by Volodymyr Skachko Kiev-Strasbourg-
Kiev; date not given: "I Already Relish the Beauty of
Shevchenko’s Testament..."]

(FBIS Translated Text] There is no doubt that Ukraine’s
joining the Council of Europe is the most significant of
recent external political events. It is an indubitable suc-
cess for Ukraine’s Supreme Council and, in particular,
for its leadership and for Borys Oliynyk’s commission
(for Foreign Affairs and Ties With the CIS]. To a large
extent, this event is associated with the name of Miguel
Angel Martinez, president of the Council of Europe Par-
liamentary Assembly, who has held this post for the last
few years. Next January, Mr. Martinez will resign after,
as he says, the tasks he was given have been largely
fulfilled.

In May 1995, during his visit to Kiev, Miguel Angel
Martinez, an ardent advocate of Russia’s admission
to the Council of Europe, acknowledged that, in the
aftermath of the events in Chechnya, he was among the
first to speak against this and switched his attention to
Ukraine, thereby recognizing the democratic character
of the transformations that are under way here.

{Oliynyk] Senor Martinez, are you pleased with the
results of your work as president of the Council of
Europe Parliamentary Assembly?

{Martinez} I belong to the school of realistic thinking
that proceeds from the fact that only what is possible
may be achieved. I tried to act accordingly, clearly
realizing what we can achieve and what is unachievable
today.

Of course, I am pleased with what we have achieved,
but I am trying to look at it objectively. This is an
encouragement for what has been achieved and a source
of self-esteem and continued progress. This supports us
in striving to achieve what we have failed to accomplish.

Speaking about the results of work over the last four or
five years, nobody expected or even dreamed about what
we managed to do over this period of time. Just four
years ago, huge numbers of people looked on European
values as a dream. Now, they have been introduced to
them and have become, so to speak, their consumers.
This is a historic success.

I am not a very reserved person and believe that, to
a large extent and in some aspects to an even greater

UKRAINE 57

than large extent, I also have something to do with
this. I am more aware than anyone what problems
lay on the road to the admission of these countries,
problems both obvious and hidden. Of course, I am
very pleased to see how many new people have passed
from one category to another. How many countries have
become upgraded from candidates to full members of
the Council of Europe and how many countries have
become candidates, for example, Russia.

Much has been done apart from a simple expansion
of the Council of Europe. We have achieved greater
progress in modernizing and expanding the doctrine of
human rights by including into it the right to culture,
economic rights, a qualitative improvement of relations
between men and women, and an improvement in ethnic
minority rights.

We also work in previously little known but no less
important spheres. Such as, for example, bioethics and
more extensive use of satellite television. The main
thing is that we have brought these concepts into line
with contemporary requirements, which they lacked
four years earlier. This, incidentally, explains why such
countries as the United States and Japan, which had
previously lived quietly for 40 years, never strived to
join the Council of Europe and ignored its activities,
turned to us a few weeks ago and asked to be given
observer status.

[Skachko] Are there any mechanisms for control over
compliance on the part of new countries that have
become members of the Council of Europe with the
organization’s requirements? Do they not change in
connection with some unusual situations that emerge
within these countries?

{Martinez} I do not generally like the word “control.”
We do not even have such a category as “new member.”
There are candidates and full members, and there
must be confident and amiable relations between them.
We proceed from the assumption that every country
that joins the Council of Europe takes upon itself an
obligation to achieve a certain progress. We want to
help it cope with this task. I would rather speak in
terms of monitoring. It is not some police system or
imposing one’s own principles. The Council of Europe
is the choice made by each country, and it does not
need to please or displease anyone. A particular country
must act in such a way as to fulfill its obligations, first
and foremost, before its own people and not to please
the Council of Europe. We are like a joint venture that
helps you, for example, to proceed along the path you
have chosen for yourselves. Besides, | do not quite
understand what “an unusual situation” means.

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

58 UKRAINE

[Skachko}] For example, the Council of Europe set
additional requirements for democratization in Turkey.

{Martinez} Greece is a better example. There was a
military coup there, the parliament was disbanded,
and Greece was compelled to leave the Council of
Europe. In Turkey, there was also a military coup in
the past, and its membership was suspended until the
situation changed. We advance the same requirements
for democratization for everybody. This is not a problem
for new members of the Council of Europe, because
Turkey is a founder country. We do not have rules for
new or old members, but we simply have common rules
for everybody.

[Skachko] Will not Ukraine’s joining the Council of
Europe change the political balance on the continent,
especially in view of what is said in Moscow about the
creation of a new Eastern European political alliance?

{Martinez} I do not think so. We are like a table where
every European country has its chair that will, sooner
or later, find its master. I always look at the political
map of Europe. Before Ukraine occupied its position at
the common table, the map looked like an incomplete
crossword. It had a big white gap that had to be filled in
so that there would not be a bigger whole through which
water and European values would leak out. Our mosaic
must be complete, otherwise it wil! not work. Ukraine’s
place in this mosaic is quite significant and I believe
that, in a few weeks, we will fill it in completely. When I
was in Kiev, one of your people asked me at the airport:
"Do you believe that next year Ukraine will become a
member of the Council of Europe?" I answered that I
would not yet be too old when Ukraine joins us. It was
a very risky statement on my part, but today I may say
that I won the bet — two bottles of champagne, and I
hope that it will be Crimean champagne.

[Skachko] Senor Martinez, what do you mean by the
concept "democratic security" which is increasingly
spoken of in Europe?

[Martinez] It is the expansion of democracy to all parts
of the world. Expansion in depth rather than in breadth
{ne vshyr, a v hlyb). It is a cultivation of democracy that
is determined by two factors. First, without democracy,
there is no security or genuine stability. When there is
no democracy in a country, it may seem to be stable.
However, this is a very illusory impression. Democracy
is the only thing that guarantees stability for the people,
because it is the only system that enables all problems to
be resolved with the participation of the people and for
their benefit. At any rate, for the benefit of the majority
of people. History has shown that if this is the case, less
mistakes are made.

FBIS-SOV-95-198
13 October 1995

Second, democracy is something that can never be ul-
timately achieved. Like at the Olympic Games where
the prevailing motto is "Faster, higher, and stronger."
Democracy also comes from one’s neighbors. Demo-
cratic security implies consolidation of democrats in the
whole world.

{Skachko] After the decision on Ukraine was adopted,
Viadimir Lukin, head of the Russian delegation, said at
a news conference that “Russia’s resentment increases
over the double standards" and accused the Council of
Europe of engaging in political games that, according to
him, take place during the admission or nonadmission of
new candidate countries. What is your attitude to this?

(Martinez) I can understand him, but I do not agree
with what he says. Of course, they are disappointed.
They hoped that Russia would be admitted in May 1995,
but the events in Chechnya had changed the situation
entirely. It is not our fault that we could not admit
Russia. It is their problem. I am personally all for
admitting Russia to the Council of Europe as soon as
possible. This would be to everybody’s advantage and I
hope that it will take place. Incidentally, 1 am also very
disappointed that such a great candidate country could
not join us during the period of my presidency.

I have two great disappointments. They concern Russia
and Bosnia-Herzegovina. Two black pages in the bulky
book of my presidency. However, I am an optimist:
Perhaps, I will no longer be president and will not be the
father when these "babies" are born, but everybody will
remember that I was there when they were conceived.
Generally speaking, the process of conception is much
more gratifying than birth proper.

[Skachko] Perhaps, Ms. Lennie Fischer, your successor
at the post of president, being a woman, understands
this better?

{Martinez} I hope that she will be a better "father." I
know, however, that I will still have to put in much
effort to have these two questions resolved positively.
One of the most unpleasant aspects of my work is that I
have to exert pressure. Incidentally, until last Monday, I
kept wondering whether Ukraine would or would not be
admitted. Once this happened, we immediately switched
our attention to Macedonia. Macedonia was admitted,
and now I must turn to Russia. Of course, this is very
tiring. This has continued throughout the 1,359 days of
my presidency and will continue for about another 100
days.

However, now, when my tenure is expiring, I already
relish the beauty of Shevchenko’s "Testament" ["Za-
povit" — poetry]. I am getting ready for a visit to Kiev.
When I was there, the weather was very nice, it was

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-95-198
13 October 1995

spring. Now, I want to visit Ukraine and look at it in
January, in winter, when it has become a full member
of the Council of Europe. Ukraine is the largest country
that has joined us during my tenure. Of course, I wish
you every success!

Kiev Asks G-7 for $3.2 Billion To Close Chernobyl

LD1210204795 Kiev UNIAN in Ukrainian
1850 GMT 12 Oct 95

{[FBIS Translated Text] Kiev 12 Oct — Ukraine has
asked the G-7 countries for a grant of $3.2 billion to
fund the closure of Chernobyl AES [nuclear electric
power station], Nur Nihmatullin, first deputy chairman
of the Ukrainian State Committee for the Use of Nuclear
Power, told UNIAN after two days of talks with G-7
experts.

The money will be used to fund projects to guarantee
safety during the closure of the nuclear plant, improve
safety provisions at the third power unit that will
remain in operation until the year 2000 and the Shelter
facility [a containment structure over the crippled fourth
reactor], and ensure the safe disposal of radioactive
waste.

Ukraine expects $2.5 billion from the West to finance
profit- making projects involved with the closure of
Chernobyl AES. These include the completion of two
new power units at Rovno and Khmelnitskiy nuclear
power plants and the restructuring of the nuclear indus-

try.

{[FBIS Transcribed Text] Ukraine is interested in a

of stability in the eastern neighbor country, President

in Kiev with a delegation of state secretaries from
Germany.

The deputy head of the presidential press service Andrey
Chirva told Interfax that the process of building new
Ukraine and new Russia is a lengthy one.

Kuchma stressed that Russia is and will remain the
main partner for Ukraine. Speaking of the Black Sea
Fleet problem he said he sent a letter to President Boris
Yeltsin calling to step up efforts at the level of prime-
ministers instead of communicating through the media.

"There is actually no work. If the situation continues, I
do not see how it can resolve the problem. And this is
not Ukraine’s fault,” he said.

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

60 UKRAINE

Kuchma was critical about the idea of a referendum
on the unification of CIS Slavic countries. In his opin-
ion, Russia and Belarus, for instance, should observe
the terms of a customs union in order to cooperate nor-
mally. "We should cooperate and understand each other
without dragging peoples into that," he remarked.

He voiced satisfaction with the approval of the govern-
ment program by parliament and stressed that Ukraine
will follow the road of reforms approved by parliament
last year.

Kuchma was Satisfied that the agreement on regular
exchanges of delegations with Germany reached during
his visit to Bonn last July is taking concrete form.
He spoke of the special relationship between Kiev and
Bonn and voiced the opinion that Germany could play
a special role in Ukraine’s life.

of the nuclear power station should be made by the end
of the year. "We need a program, not a concept,” he
stressed.

if the West helps build substituting power stations and
resolve personnel problems.)

Kohl planned for next year.

and the migration of ethnic Germans from other CIS
countries to Ukraine.

(FBIS Translated Text] Ukraine’s interparliamentary
ties are gradually expanding. This is also promoted
by Ukraine’s Supreme Council Commission for For-
eign Affairs and Ties With the CIS. Last week,
Borys Oliynyk, head of the commission, and the
deputy chairman of Kuwait’s National Congress,
who headed a Kuwaiti delegation to Kiev, held a joint
news conference.

Ukraine’s interparliamentary activity is gradually ad-
vancing to the east. Of course, this does not signify our
politicians’ desire iv abandon ihe iargeiy expiored Wesi-
ern regions. However, the “breakthrough at the East-
ern front" has opened up new potential possibilities for
Ukraine.

By contrast to bilateral relations at a governmental level,
interparliamentary ties are just gaining momentum. The
visit by the delegation of Kuwait's National Congress
is the first meeting of our people’s deputies with
parliamentarians of a friendly state.

Kuwait is primarily known in the world for its crude
oil. Ukraine also expects, in the near future, to import
Kuwaiti “black gold.” During the meeting between
Salekh Yussef al-Fadal [name as transliterated], head
of Kuwait's National Congress, and Ukraine's Supreme
Council Chairman Oleksandr Moroz, they discussed a

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-95-198
13 October 1995

possibility of oil deliveries by tankers to Odessa where
an oil terminal is under construction.

Kuwait is perfectly capable of helping Ukraine avoid a
crisis situation with regard to energy sources, consider-
ing that its explored oil reserves account for 10 percent
of world ones. The price of oil is $17.5 per barrel. Alto-
gether 920 oil wells work for the economy of this Near
Eastern country. Kuwait extracts 800,000 barrels of oil
a day. More accurately, it used to extract... As a re-
sult of the Iraqi aggression, Kuwait’s national economy
complex was virtually totally destroyed. According to
official estimates, the total volume of losses amounts to
between $60 billion and $100 billion. The oil sector suf-
fered the most. Seven hundred and thirty oil wells were
put on fire. The operation on extinguishing the fire alone
cost $2 billion. Despite protests and resolutions passed
by the UN Security Council that recommended that Iraq
return to Kuwait the looted material values, compensate
for losses, and release prisoners of war, this has not been
done to this day. Six hundred and twenty-five Kuwaiti
citizens are still held in Iraqi prisons.

During the Kuwaiti delegation’s visit, the Ukrainian side
undertook to support this state within the framework of
the activity of the International Parliamentary Union,
help in release of imprisoned Kuwaiti citizens, and insist
that Iraq fulfill all international resolutions. Kuwait, in
turn, will make efforts to secure the release of Ukrainian
POW’s held in Afghanistan. Supreme Council deputies
got an invitation to visit Kuwait. Not bad for a start.

Prime Minister Marchuk on Government Program

LD1210232595 Kiev Radio Ukraine World Service
in Ukrainian 1710 GMT 12 Sep 95

[Report on news conference with Prime Minister
Yevheniy Marchuk by correspondent Oleksy Havrysh
in Kiev on 12 October; Marchuk’s remarks recordcdj

(FBIS Translated Excerpt) [Havrysh] Most of the news
conference was devoted to the government’s program
of action — which was approved in parliament on 11
October — the debate on the program, and various
issues that were raised during the debate. [passage
omitted]

The left-wingers failed to convince the government as
they put forward their main argument: The Cabinet
of Ministers cooperates with international financial
institutions that allegedly want to buy Ukraine in bulk
and by retail. We have been cooperating and we will
be cooperating [with international financial institutions],
the prime minister retorted.

UKRAINE 61

(Marchuk] We have been cooperating and we will be
cooperating with international financial institutions, in
spite of any criticism and no matter where it comes
from. One of the reasons for this is the fact that Ukraine
has found itself, as a result of previous management,
in an extremely difficult situation. Without attracting
serious, rich foreign investors, it will be very difficult
for us to get out of the deep crisis we find ourselves
in, especially in some industries. There should be no
illusions here. There is another question. There are fears
that they [international financial institutions} will buy
{Ukraine}, plunder it and so on. Of course no one will
ever allow this to happen. Although, at a time when our
budget deficit for next year is planned at the level of
6 percent, when our debts stand at $8.8 billion — and
these are heavy and complicated debts — and when
we are facing a reform of the entire power engineering
system — I would not like to take your time, but if
you are interested to know I will tell you that dozens
of billions of dollars are required. We cannot afford to
spend that amount of money.

The coal industry, the power engineering industry as a
whole, and a closed-cycle production of fuel for nuclear
power stations require more than $1 billion. We do not
have monetary resources for this. There are many other
spheres [that need investments}: the reform of the agro-
industrial complex is one of these. It is clear that, first
and foremost, we will definitely cooperate and obtain
credits so as not to neutralize or suppress the domestic
producer but, on the contrary, to create production
cycles in the country. However, this should be done by
means of attracting foreign credits and foreign investors.
[passage omitted]

of other urgent problems. This is what he thinks about
progress in the talks with the G-7 leaders on the closure
of the Chernobyl nuclear electric power station [AES]:

[Marchnk! The issne is not a simple one, and ta some
extent we have complicated it ourselves. By now, we

was a Situation when the minister would say one thing,
the committee another, and the plant director something
different. By now we have developed a state policy on
the matter, which is being outlined in the president's
statements.

I think the figure of 4 billion [dollars] should not have
been mentioned. The closure of Chernobyl AES may
cost more or may cost less, but the figure of 4 billion
will scare off anyone. I had a long talk with U'S.
Vice President Al Gore, as well as with the European
Union leaders. As you know, the Ukrainian president
has written to the G-7 leaders stressing that we are

promising. Besides, this is the use of wind-powered

UKRAINE 63

already functioning at Donuzlav [a harbor used as a

functioning well enough and rather effectively. The

also has good prospects for the future, although it is
clear that one should not count on them [generators] as
the main substitute for other sources of electric power.
However, this is a good supplement in conditions of the
shortage of electricity here in Ukraine.

it has not yet been completed — we are creating our
own closed-cycle production of fuel for nuclear power
stations. This is a very large-scale and expensive project.

well-known U.S. firm Westinghouse and others to take
part in the tender. We will not be able to implement the
project independently; howevez, we will have our own
cycle for the production of nuclear fuel for our nuclear
power stations.

As regards other trends — solar energy and so on
— work is being done toward that end. However, the
most important, as I have already said, is nuclear power
engineering, which now accounts for 30 percent of the
total volume of electricity produced. We are planning to
increase this figure to 40 percent. Next go thermal power
stations and hydroelectric power stations. While in the
United States, we signed an agreement with the World
Bank on granting Ukraine a credit of $115 million on
favorable terms for the modernization and rehabilitation
of our hydroelectric power stations. Their potentials
have not yet been used here in Ukraine.

Kuchma Hails Parliament’s Approval of
Government Program

VII 1N290S28S Mascaw INTERE
ERPS LEVLUE IIMS PEOSCOW LEVI LAS

1805 GMT 12 Oct 95

{From the "Presidential Bulletin" feature: compiled by
Nikolay Zherebtsov and Andrey Petrovskiy; edited by
Viadimir Shishlin]

{[FBIS Transcribed Text] The parliamentary approval
of the government program is a very positive step
that in some sense gives confidence to the whole
country, President Leonid Kuchma told the press in Kiev
Thursday, the day following the parliamentary decision
which he described as fateful.

In Kuchma’s opinion the resolution demonstrated that
there is a majority capable of decision-making in
parliament. On the whole Kuchma called the approval

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

oad UKRAINE

a major event and a holiday for Ukraine, a day when it
became clear to everyone that the country will continue
advancing along the road of reform.

IF Note: On Wednesday evening the Ukrainian parlia-
ment approved the cabinet program of action on the
whole. It instructed the government to take into account
the proposals and remarks made during the debate and
submit a plan of concrete steps simultaneously with the
draft of the 1996 budget.

During the roll call on the resolution submitted by eight
groups and factions it was supported by 234 deputies.
61 out of 323 attending deputies voted against and 17
abstained.

In an exclusive interview with Interfax Wednesday
Kuchma was optimistic about the upcoming resolution
and assumed that the program would have the general
approval and the cabinet would get a chance to imple-
ment it.

However, in his opinion the mixed and politically
unstructured parliament would not be content with any
program. Kuchma called absolutely impermissible the
actual delivery of an alternative program by Natalya
Vitrenko during the program debate.

IF Note: On the pretext of expressing a special opin-
ion, as speaker Oleksandr Moroz put it, Vitrenko made
a Statement in which she actually described the funda-
mentals of an alternative economic program worked out
by the Socialist Party.

According to her, the main difference of the Socialist
program was the renunciation of privatization in indus-
try. The maximum profit tax should not exceed 45 per-
cent. Besides, the Socialist program implied a tax recess
for priority productions.

Vitrenko was convinced that the Socialist program
would triple company profits, double actual earnings in
industry and reduce prices by one third.

In the president’s opinion the parliamentary debate also
demonstrated the unacceptability of Moroz combining
the posts of parliamentary speaker and leader of the
Socialist Party.

IF Note: At the Wednesday evening session Moroz
said he was categorically opposed to making land a
commodity. In his opinion the government program
contains an ill-disguised assumption of that.

Moroz called trade in land a crime against Ukraine.
"I would insist on dropping these paragraphs from the
program because they are a threat to the future," the
speaker said.

FBIS-SOV-95-198
13 October 1995

Prime-Minister Yevheniy Marchuk calied Vitrenko’s
speech a planned provocation against the government.

After the speech he told deputies that the announcement
of an alternative program at the session is not provided
for either the constitutional agreement, or the regulations
of parliament.

Before the government program was put to vote
Marchuk made his final speech and described as
the objectives of his cabinet a balanced budget and
support for production. The prime-minister described
the program as a foundation for guaranteeing true
advancement.

He stressed that settlements with Russia have been or-
ganized during the past year and the Russian positive
attitude has helped restructure the debts for gas deliver-
ies.

According to the prime-minister, Ukraine’s foreign debt
amounts to $8.8 billion. "The IMF is helping us in
settlements for the debts and thus working for the
government,” Marchuk declared.

He gave a news conference in Kiev Thursday where he
announced that the cabinet would immediately pass a
resolution to enforce the program after finalizing it.

He said the enforcement mechanism would contain a
concrete schedule and a list of responsible ministers.
Marchuk stressed that the enforcement of the program
largely depends on its implementation in the provinces.

The prime-minister announced that cabinet members
would meet Thursday the leaders of eight parliamentary
groups that supported the program. He also intended
to propose the formation of a government alliance in
parliament. He specified that it would be an alliance
supporting economic reforms.

In reply to a question whether he would head the
alliance, if elected to parliament (Marchuk has been
nominated candidate in Poltava region - IF), the prime-
minister said he has aot met voters or decided whether
he would run in elections.

Presidential economic advisor Anatoliy Halchinskyy
told Interfax the parliamentary resolution is a confir-
mation of the course of amending economic reforms
proclaimed by Kuchma last April. According to him,
the government program is a concentrated reflection of
this course aimed at an active industrial and social pol-
icy on the basis of financial stabilization.

The support of the IMF for the efforts of the government
to carry out the course is an indicative fact, the advisor
said.

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-95-198
13 October 1995

"All that proves that the presidential course is balanced
and optimal and meets the political as well as economic
realities of the day,” Halchinskyy said.

Deputy Prime-Minister in charge of economy Roman
Shpek named as the main distinctive feature of the cab-
inet program its concentration on achieving economic
growth on a realistic basis.

In an interview with Interfax he said the program is
built on the foundation of financial stabilization and the
transition of the emphasis to the micro level. In Shpek’s
opinion industrial growth could be achieved in 1996.

"We count on financial stabilization. This will result in
a decline in lending rates. Companies will get a chance
to accumulate profits and turnover capital will not lose
its value due to inflation. This will give an impetus to
economic activities," the deputy prime-minister said.

Shpek also said that in 1996 the government intends
to draw about $2.5 billion in loans from international
financial organizations as well as loans on a bilateral
basis.

Kuchma Rejects Cooperation With Supreme
Council

LD1210190395 Kiev UNIAN in Ukrainian
1700 GMT 12 Oct 95

{[FBIS Translated Text] Kiev — There will never be
any cooperation with the Supreme Council majority,
said Ukrainian President Leonid Kuchma at a meeting
with representatives of Ukrainian youth organizations.
The president was commenting on the parliament’s vote
earlier today to raise the low-income threshold.

Ukrainian Prime Minister Yevhen Marchuk told the
Supreme Council yesterday that such a move would
require 500 trillion karbovantsi, more than the state
could afford. According to President Kuchma, today’s
resolution was a populist act designed to discredit
executive authorities and show off the parliament as the
only one concerned about people’s well-being.

Economic Issues
Marchuk Presents Economic Program

LD1210144095 Kiev Radio Ukraine World Service
in Ukrainian 1915 GMT I1 Oct 95

[Address by Prime Minister Yevhen Marchuk to
Ukrainian Supreme Council session in Kiev on 11
October — recorded]

(FBIS Translated Text] In accordance with the consti-
tutional treaty, the Ukrainian Cabinet of Ministers has
submitted a program of its activities to the Supreme
Council. The analysis of the socioeconomic situation in

UKRAINE 65

the country reveals its exceptionally complex and con-
tradictory nature. On the one hand, there are grounds
for stating, cautiously, that there have been certain im-
provements for the better connected with the initial and
the most difficult stage of reforms. I have in mind, first
of all, the practical steps toward the establishment of a
market economy as a functionally complete system and
a Strategic aim of the policy that is being implemented.

The non state sector of the economy is developing. The
creation of a diversified structure of privatization has
given it more dynamism and helped to make the process
more manageable. Budgetary relations are being formed
through the delimitation of powers at the centre and at
local levels and the state budget is being freed from
the functions that are not characteristic of it. The rate of
increase in the country’s foreign trade turnover is rising.
Exports are on the increase despite the general decline
in Output. The structure of foreign trade and interstate
economic relations is improving. The exchange rate has
been unified and the currency market is developing. The
establishment of the banking system and a network of
financial agents is nearing completion. About 60 general
state and sectoral programmes are being drawn up and
implemented. They are aimed at resolving important
economic tasks, above all energy conservation and the
construction of airplanes, missiles and shipbuilding, the
production of Ukrainian electric locomotives, trams,
trolleybuses and buses. On the whole, it is possible to
say that we have created a framework for stabilization,
and for the first time this has been achieved by economic
means of regulation.

However, we have not succeeded in strengthening the
positive tendencies and making them irreversible. More-
over, notable signs of economic and social instability
continue to exist against this background. That which
we have achieved has had little effect on the health
of the national economy and, first of all, production as
its main component, and, in the long run, on the liv-
ing conditions of the people, on their well-being. The
reality of the present moment is the continuing, albeit
slowing down considerably, decline in output, the wind-
ing down of business and investment activity, the de-
teriorating situation in the fuel and energy complex,
the growth in the negative balance of payments, the
criminalization of the economy and the administrative
spheres. The insufficient feedback from the measures
taken to restructure the market economy was caused by
the fact that attention was usually concentrated on indi-
vidual issues without a properly synchronized approach
to their resolution, taking into account their priorities,
mutual relations, possible consequences and the over-
all effect on the socioeconomic situation on the whole,
and, apart from that, the degree of the economy’s readi-

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

66 UKRAINE

ness to accept these or those innovations. Thus, price
liberalization, being a forced measure to a great extent,
came ahead of the establishment of a competitive en-
vironment. The currency exchange regulation was im-
plemented in the absence of an established legislative
framework. Changes in the tax policy were not fully
backed with a single system of taxes, duties and manda-
tory payments and clear procedures for their collection
into the budget. State subsidies were a common prac-
tice, along with the funding of expenses without taking
into consideration the available budget resources.

Without the required minimum conditions and, first of
all, the radical reforming of ownership relations, the
state and its institutions were increasingly removed from
the regulation and control of the most important com-
ponents and factors of the national economy. Unfor-
tunately the processes that were taking place in many
aspects of the economy were not heeded and this pre-
vented us from making a clear forecast of develop-
ment scenarios and the socioeconomic consequences,
but most importantly, from making appropriate and
timely adjustments.

This runs counter to the generally accepted and proven
world experience, which teaches that the management
of macroeconomic processes during transitional periods
and crises cannot be carried out without strong, firm and
resolute state power. Ukraine has suffered a significant
economic slump and losses and has been almost con-
stantly in a state of hyperinflation. According to some
estimates, this period for us has been the longest in
the 20th Century. The economy has been highly defi-
cient while there have been virtually no nonemissionary
sources of covering the deficit. The investment shortage
has forced our country out of the world market of highly
technical and scientific goods and left it to consume its
own national wealth.

Because of these conditions we have tried to devise
the government action program not as a compilation of
generally known tasks but as an action instrument for
choosing the priorities, ways and means of stopping and
overcoming the crisis, creating the necessary conditions
for further transformations in the economy and improv-
ing people’s lives. In a wider scope, the program, as I
said, provides a practical basis for the implementation
of the conceptual principles of the Ukrainian president’s
economic policy, announced in October 1994, and the
correction of reforms, the need for which was stated in
the president’s later statements.

The ideology of this document is based on the constitu-
tional treaty, specifically, Article 31, which defines the
range of the Cabinet of Ministers’ duties and powers.
In the course of its finalization, we also considered the

FBIS-SOV-95-198
13 October 1995

recommendations put forward by the all-Ukrainian con-
ference of economists held in September this year.

The program is aimed first of all at the comprehensive
integration of urgent practical tasks and the development
of a single anticrisis strategy on that basis.

The government sees its main task in ensuring economic
growth as a crucial condition of improving the situation
in the country, first of all, in the social sphere. A system
of the following mutually related economic measures is
designed to achieve that aim:

1. Substantial reduction in the rate of inflation from 7.5
percent on average a month this year to 2.4-2.5 percent
next year. Among other things, it will make access to
credit resources considerably easier.

2. Boosting of the investment process and, most impor-
tantly, encouraging long-term capital investment into the
production sphere.

3. A structural and industrial policy which would ensure
the priority development of the industries working for
the domestic as well as the outside market and providing
a fast capital turnover.

4. Overcoming the payment crisis. A package of mea-
sures, outlined by the instruction of the president of
Ukraine, has been developed for that purpose. In par-
ticular, it is envisaged to improve the circulation of
promissory notes and the state regulation of prices for
the products of monopoly enterprises.

5. Relieving the general tax pressure to encourage an
increase in business activity. The rate of GDP redistri-
bution through the budget, together with payments to
the pension fund, will be decreased to 45-47 percent.

6. Improving the balance of payments, first of all, by
stimulating export, and introducing changes to the fuel
import and payment procedure and the implementation
of energy and resource saving programs.

In the long run, our aim is to stabilize the gross domestic
product, ensure its growth and, on this basis, improve
the living standards.

Later I shal! speak about it in greater detail, but now
I would like to draw your attention to another special
feature of our program. Its content and general tone
is determined by the premise that reforms should not
be excessively costly or achieved at any price. The
price of reform in general, and the social price in
particular, is a category which determines our entire
future. We should be realistic and clearly see the
main contradiction which adds to the difficulties of our
transition, that is the imbalance between the limited
resources of the state, especially the financial ones,

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-95-198
13 October 1995

and the scale and complexity of the problems at hand.
Given such conditions, the maximalist principle of here
and now is unrealistic. It cannot be sustained even
by more stable and regulated economic systems. In its
program, the government did not strive to squeeze life
into abstract, preconstructed schemes, much less to play
up to potential opponents. Its essence is an effort to
achieve a concrete and real practical result.

In the overall context of macro-economic stabilization,
the primary significance is attached to the growth of
real GDP. It is an integral factor which characterizes
the efficiency of the economic system on the whole
and crucially affects all spheres of social life. Its
improvement is the subject of all sections in the
economic part of the program, in this way or another.
This affects, first of all, the budget and taxation policy.
The government is placing the main emphasis on
achieving an increase in budget revenue. This does not
mean increasing the tax pressure, but ensuring that all
contributions payable to the budget are received on time
and in full. Of no less importance is the reduction of
excessive, unreasonable expenditure which the state is
not able to sustain. The government will propose a
temporary freeze, for a year or two, on some of the
programs we are unable to fund properly.

I should add that the government is working together
with the Supreme Council on the reform of the entire tax
system. We want to make it more simple and, mainly,
stable, ensuring an equal burden on all taxpayers and a
more favourable climate for business activity. We are
also considering the possibility of introducing, where
justified, a tax payable in kind, or in a mixed form.
With regard to the relation between this problem and
the production sphere, there is a direct connection. The
growth of production, together with an increase in sales
results in more revenue to the budget.

At the same time, the government will work to achieve
a steady and smooth procedure for the payment of taxes
into the budget, so that those evading this duty to the
State would not be merely getting off with a fright. Such
measures will be tough but inevitable. I shall quote just
a few figures. Almost two-thirds of businesses which
underwent inspection in the first eight months of this
year were found in breach of the tax legislation. As a
result of inspections, some 50,000 billion karbovantsi
were collected for the state budget. This is 150 percent
more than the budget expenditure for science, or half
the entire spending on defence. This is the price of the
Situation. And this is only what has been uncovered.
On the whole, some 40 percent of payable budget
contributions are not being received. Think of how many
problems, including social ones, could be solved if these
funds were received by the state treasury. Unfortunately,

UKRAINE 67

there is only one new law enacted in this field so far,
the Law on Enterprise Profit Tax, and it already requires
amendment. Other acts from the tax package are still at
the drafting stage, having formed the basis of the state
budget for the second year in a row.

The core of the work to cure the economy is moving to
the regions and the workforce. That is why the reform
of budgetary relations will continue with the division
of power between the center and the local authorities.
This is an important aspect of regional economic policy,
the concept of which was approved by the Cabinet of
Ministers. At the same time, the government will treat as
especially unacceptable and intolerable any incidents of
high-handedness in the local distribution of the budget.
Since the budget is the main financial plan of the state,
stretching the budget will lead directly to its fall. And
this will affect not only the economy but the politics
as well. This is where the state treasury system comes
in, which we hope will start working this year. One of
its important functions will be control over the use of
budget funds. This will prevent the spending of budget
allocations at will. Such tendencies, unfortunately, do
exist. An inspection, carried out by the Cabinet of
Ministers, revealed that one trillion karbovantsi worth
of credits and loans were spent on purposes different
from those for which they were issued.

The payment crisis is also having a negative effect on
production and the process of financial stabilization. It
has turned into a real plague for our economy, hav-
ing reached truly astronomical proportions. Accounts
receivable by Ukrainian enterprises stood at 1 Septem-
ber 1995 at nearly 1.7 trillion karbovantsi and accounts
payable exceeded 2 trillion karbovantsi, which is larger
than the entire gross domestic product [GDP]. For that
reason the budget failed to receive on time nearly 80
trillion karbovantsi. In accordance with a relevant in-
struction by the president of Ukraine, the Cabinet of
Ministers is introducing a procedure under which enter-
prises will sell their goods only to solvent customers,
who can guarantee payment or who can provide secu-
rity such as property. For this purpose we will activate a
mechanism of the mandatory issue of promissory notes
to settle debts, including the debts accumulated earlier,
with the actual use of bankruptcy procedure and con-
version of financial obligations into property liabilities.

I believe this constructive outline of problems and tasks
gives an idea about the extreme complexity of the
situation in which the government is setting out to step
up the financial stabilization, the structural changes, the
slowing down of the decline, and increasing production
and the GDP. Next year, we expect to maintain the GDP
virtually at this year’s levei. The itcrease in industrial
output is planned within 0.6 percent, that of agricultural

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

68 UKRAINE

production within 0.2 percent. These targets should not
be seen as excessively modest. First of all, we clearly
see and would like to use the opportunities for their
achievement, that is, do what we have been unable to do
in the past four years. Second, it is such improvements,
although gradual and not always noticeable, that are
incomparably more valuable than the torrents of ritual
economic rhetoric which has been the background for
the continuous decline of these indicators.

The certainty and the feasibility of this task and the fu-
ture increase in GDP are based on calculations and fore-
casts. We are talking about real support for Ukrainian
producers. The government will make available the nec-
essary conditions for the normal functioning of the pro-
ductive sphere through improving the legislative base,
perfecting the budget and taxation policy, and protec-
tion from unfair competition in the domestic market.

The resolution of specific economic issues is now being
placed on the businesses themselves. They should take
charge of expanding the market for their output, set-
ting payments with suppliers and consumers, looking
for sources of investment in production instead of ex-
pecting traditional injections, subsidies, and preferential
credits and tax benefits from the budget. Obviously such
a reorientation cannot be painless. It requires a break
with old- fashioned psychological stereotypes. There is
no turning back. This is a necessary precondition for
adapting to market realities. We are also proceeding
from the need to reduce the volume of nonindustrial
consumption which at the moment is excessively high
and at the expense of this increasing industrial consump-
tion. To put it more simply, we must not consume most
of what is created but channel it into the productive
sphere for its dynamic development and strengthen the
financial base for economic reforms.

It is worth adding that according to valuations, the vol-
ume of GDP is now at the level it was in the mid-1970s.
Meanwhile, the appetite of distribution and especially
consumption continues to increase as if nothing hap-
pened. Realization of the importance of this problem
at the micro level, that is, at the enterprises themselves,
will be insured not by administrative intervention nor by
directives from above. The government intends to make
use of such conditions and stimuli that would realisti-
cally interest workers’ collectives not only to increase
their income but first and foremost to channel a crucial
part of it to generating working capital, to expand and
technologically restruct output.

In addition, a routine has already been established which
links the volumes of the consumption fund at enterprises
with the results of their activities. By no means does this
signify the freezing or reductions in salaries. On the

FBIS-SOV-95-198
13 October 1995

contrary, collectives that work efficiently will increase
the amount of goods sold and receive an opportunity
to increase their salaries. It is necessary to note that
the need for adjustment is dictated today by the low
level of salaries which is caused first and foremost by
the unprecedented low level of output. The permanent
deficit in the investment of capital in the productive
sphere, as well as most other difficuities in the economy,
is caused by inflation and we have succeeded in
completely overcoming it. Calculations demonstrate that
its reduction to at least 30 percent a year, although even
in our conditions this is for the time being very difficult,
would enable us to normalize the financial state of
enterprises and increase investment activity and output.

In its program the government did not restrict itself to
the traditional view for the reasons of inflation which
is based on excessive demand for money. The main
means of fighting inflation is to reduce state expendi-
ture, obtain additional stabilization credits from interna-
tional financial organizations, and expand the securities
market. It was also taken into account that inflation in
Ukraine was caused by the technological backwardness
of production, the high material, labor, and energy in-
tensiveness of output, and its corresponding low quality
as well as by the monopolism of producers and the low
level of use of productive capacities. To counteract this
exclusively by means of restricting aggregate demand
is not effective because, unfortunately, enterprises fa-
vor reducing output and continue to raise the price of
their products. What you have is a closed circle. The
decline in output is one of the reasons for inflation and
in turn it increases inflationary pressures itself because
it Causes price rises. On the other hand the reduction in
budget revenues compels us to print more money.

In order to break this chain the main stake is made
on stabilization of prices as a precondition of further
increase in production and investment. The develop-
ment of an optimum corridor in which a strict finan-
cial policy would unite with priority financing of the
most promising investment projects, including at the
cost of the budget within emissions, although planned
ones, is also at issue. Rational substantiation of direction
of these resources to the material base of the economy
and modern technologies will also lead to expansion of
volumes of sales and thus to speeding up the move-
ment of commodities. This will stimulate not a primi-
tive feudal barter system but highly organized effective
economic activities along the classical market principle
with a civilized process of buying and selling in the cen-
ter and not a primitive barter exchange. These measures
will have in their essence and consequences a clearly
defined antiinflationary nature. We attribute fundamen-
tal importance not to a total financial rescue of enter-

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-95-198
13 October 1995

prises, to which some people are calling us now, but to
a selective purposeful investment. However, the state is
short of funds to provide fully even for such a selective
approach. The government will not and cannot support
ineffective enterprises which are effectively bankrupt.
The main criteria in the selection of objects for invest-
ment will be the following: Solving the issue of energy
saving, energy production, and alternative sources of
energy supply; production of export-worthy and import-
replacing goods and also production of goods of special
social importance, that is, medicines, food, and other ba-
sic goods; the ability of producers to find and to expand
markets for their goods in the country and abroad, and
to increase accordingly the volumes of sales; possibil-
ities for speeding up production in adjacent industries,
in the entire manufacturing complex and for stimulat-
ing full cycles of production cooperation. Intensifying
the processes of investment will create favorable condi-
tions for the introduction of a regime which will insure
that credits reach their addressees directly without mid-
dle men, without loan agencies, and will insure their
likewise direct and unconditional return.

Private capital and new forms of mixed investment,
that is, state and private, are a prospective reserve
which the government plans to involve in the investment
sphere. I will also draw your attention to the fact
that because of the current relatively high rate of
inflation the government, with the help of the National
Bank, developed a mechanism of encouragement of
investment activities of commercial banks. In particular,
if the share of long-term credits makes up at least 25
percent of the total volume of the credit portfolio, such
banks will obtain credit support and they will benefit
from more favorable norms of economic activities.
It is planned to remove restrictions on direct bank
investments at the cost of private capital. The complex
of tasks for development of securities market includes
creating conditions for participation in it of commercial
banks.

We are turning, effectively for the first time, to the prac-
tice of crediting projects by consortiums mostly through
concentrating resources of several banks on the condi-
tions of joint responsibility. Insuring implementation of
such projects reduces at the same time the risk that cred-
its will not be paid back. It is clear that strict control aud
a system of economic sanctions are always necessary in
the case of purpose-linked credits in order to insure that
the allocated funds should be used exclusively for their
specified purpose. Such a mechanism is already being
developed.

Our special attention remains focused on the substantial
drop in labor productivity. This on is, one hand, a re-
sult of the economic crisis and on the other hand it is

UKRAINE 69

a negative factor which aggravates it. Unfortunately, it
seems that this problem has not particularly interested
anybody. It was believed that it was not especially im-
portant from the point of view of macroeconomic sta-
bilization. Meanwhile, over the past four years labor
productivity in Ukraine has almost halved. The most
obvious reason for that is that the production slump
was not followed by an adequate reduction in the num-
ber of employees. However, it is not just due to simple
arithmetic. There were a whole range of interconnected
factors, the most crucial of which was the implementa-
tion of structural transformations, effective conservation
of old cooperation links because of new more effective
ones, the spending nature of the economy, the objective
unfavorable conditions for restoring production and in-
creasing its technological, organizational, and rational-
ization level.

I should talk separately about the labor legislation
and the system of payment for work. The balance
between the real results of work and payment for it
has been distorted. Even those imperfect levers which
made it possible to maintain that balance on a more
or less acceptable level have been discarded. One can
speak in general about the ruining of the whole system
of payment for work. An unacceptable disproportion
has formed not only between industries but within
those industries too. The gap between the levels of
income from different types of activities is growing, for
example, in banking and intermediary spheres on the
one hand and in the manufacturing and public spheres
on the other. Such deformations and distortions have
virtually destroyed work motivation and are intensifying
social tension. On final analysis, wages do not provide
for normal regeneration of labor and on the other hand
they also continue to grow outside the context of the
real production effectiveness.

For those reasons the government aims to increase the
role and importance of tariffs in the structure of wages,
to regulate the impact of the main and additional wages
on the cost of goods and services. We plan to sort out
the procedure for paying wages in the next few months
and to raise Le level of responsibility of administration
for delays in the payment of wages. From 1996 onward
the reform of the entire system of wages will begin and
conditions for increasing real income of the population
will be created. In simpler terms, people will have a
chance to work normally and earn adequately. And to
those who have no such chance, that is, pensioners,
disabled people, and other categories of the population
who are not able to work, the state will guarantee real
support. The reforms in the system of pension provision
and social insurance are geared toward that.

70 UKRAINE

The government is now preparing proposals to introduce
relevant changes to the legislation on pensions. I would
like to dwell separately on those problems that not a
single member of the government as well as all of you,
esteemed people’s deputies, can ignore. I have in mind
the poverty level, more precisely its size. Last week
this again became the focus of intense discussion in
parliament. We are also in favor of every person being
able to satisfy his more important requirements. But
again we must proceed from the real possibilities of
the state. One could make a separate speech on this but
I will only dwell on the individual and most obvious
results which could lead to approval of the poverty level
at 4.8 million karbovantsi a month.

First, by the end of the year we would need in
excess of 690 trillion karbovantsi just for salaries for
state financed institutions and pensions. Second, the
enterprises’ wage fund would increase by 200 trillion
karbovantsi. Where can we obtain these funds if we
often do not even have enough money for now?
Calculations show that however hard we try to make
ends meet we would still need to shed about 5 million
workers. Third, cash in circulation would increase by
almost 600 trillion karbovantsi thus creating a new wave
of inflation owing to which prices in December this year
would increase by 64 percent compared to September.
Fourth, even if we stop all other expenditures from the
budget then after this we would not have the funds to
finance expenditures connected with these increases.

This is not simply theory and not an attempt to frighten
somebody. Let us remember what the numerous mea-
sures in the social sphere in recent years have led to.
They were adopted with the best of intentions but with-
out sufficient economic grounds. As a result of this,
people spent more than they received. The government
cannot, should not, and has no right to deceive itself or
the people. We see a way out elsewhere: a consistent
but real, that is, an economically well grounded way
of raising people’s incomes. These are not simply in-
tentions. A package of government decisions has just
been published on wages, pensions provision, payment
of compensation, and assistance. Wages for a consider-
able section of workers in the state financed sector have
increased from 1 October by 22 percent. We hope that
the Supreme Council will support the government's pro-
posals with regard to increasing average aggregate in-
comes which would enable people to receive assistance.

In addition, decisions are being prepared that would
give the badly off a one-time cash payment for the
fall-winter period. This is roughly what was done last
year. Obviously, all of this is not yet a great deal of
money. But it is reai money and it will give the people

FBIS-SOV-95-198
13 October 1995

something rather than being burned up without trace in
the inflationary fire.

I would also like to add that a system of targeted help
and subsidies exists in the state which the government
will continue to improve. Through it we are now
helping families whose expenditures on utilities exceed
15 percent of their incomes.

There is one more extremely important socioeconomic
aspect to this work: an increase in the people’s real
incomes raises solvent demand and provides a tax
impulse and an additional impulse to output.

In this context special significance is attributed to
qualitative changes in the pricing policy. A system
of issuing every three months for enterprises price
guidelines that take into account forecast dynamics of
inflation and retail prices will be introduced already by
the end of this year.

The focus of particular attention is on the financing pro-
cesses in basic industries that remain monopolists: the
fuel and energy complex, the metallurgy, the machine-
building, the power engineering, and the transport. The
margins, formed there, can be felt everywhere down the
production chain. To avoid [word indistinct] dispropor-
tions between industries and other unpleasant surprises,
preliminary coordination of terms and levels of those
prices in the specified industries is being introduced.
This should have a positive effect on the overall price
situation in the country. Counteraction to incidents of
monopolist and discriminatory prices in the production,
supply, and trade sphere remains to be a topical task. It
is the price dictate of monopolists and ungodly profits
from resale of goods through a chain of intermediary
structures that attack the pocket of a customer most of
all. Fighting this is one of the main functions of the An-
timonopoly Committee. As for the free price creation,
its sphere will expand mostly among non-monopolized
sectors of economy, where excessive price appetites will
be restrained by competition.

The government is prepared to cooperate with trade
unions and public organizations in the field of price
control, like in other issues, which are connected with
the interests of consumers. I would like to illustrate
the complexity and diversity of the tasks that the
government has to solve by the example of the energy
supply, which is one of the most painful problems and
directly affects the interests of our national security.
The shortage of energy resources is caused not by the
fact that there is nowhere to buy them from but by the
chronic deficit of funds. Today it is one of the most
exhausting items of our import, which represents the
main burden on the payment balance and increases its
negative balance.
